# Changelog

## Unreleased

## 0.1.0-beta.33 (2026-11-10)

- Every console page is on the design system, and a test keeps it there. Text sizes are the type scale's steps, colours and layers are tokens, and buttons, fields, tabs, the navigation, error boxes and the connection strip are the shared primitives; `src/design-system.sources.test.ts` fails a line that writes a literal colour, a size off the scale or a hand-built control, and says what to write instead. What you see change: the sign-in page follows the light or dark theme instead of being always dark; orange text and icons are the darker `primary-text` in the light theme, so they pass 4.5:1; every field has an edge that is 3:1 against the page and takes the focus ring; a read that failed shows in one error banner with its Try again button; tabs move with the arrow keys; a page image opens in a dialog that holds the keyboard and has a Close button.
- The console's Runs and Triggers pages say what to do on a server that has none yet. Runs says what a run is and offers the way to the first one: Start a run opens the Playground, and Browse templates opens the templates. Triggers names the three things that can start a run (a schedule, a webhook, GitHub), each with its own button; the page's three buttons show at the top once there is a trigger. A filter that matches nothing still says so in one line.
- A `runs.wait` (or any call with a deadline) whose timer fires a fraction of a millisecond before the clock reads the deadline now ends in `AgentRunTimeout`, never `AgentRunRefused` or `AgentRunUnreachable`. The deadline is reached when its own timer fires, not only when `Date.now()` says so.
  - Upgrading: nothing to change.
- A Docker `create` aborted during a `docker run` (the egress proxy's, or the computer's own) no longer leaves a container Created behind. The abort kills the docker CLI, but the Docker daemon can still finish making the container a moment later, after the single `docker rm` of create's cleanup found nothing; the janitor took it only at a later sweep. Now, after a `docker run` that did not end with an exit of its own, the provider removes the container by name, with its anonymous volumes (and the egress volume), then looks again, every 500 ms on a bound of its own of 10 s (never the caller's, already aborted), until the looks have found none of them for 3 s in a row. The error still says create was aborted, and what was removed or could not be, always by name. A create later than that is still the janitor's sweep's, as before. A `docker run` that ended with an exit of its own is cleaned up as before.
- The console's Playground on a server with no agents shows how to make the first one. The agent picker opened a blank field over an empty list, above a form that could not send. Now a panel offers Create an agent and Browse templates, a read-only list names what a request takes, and the code samples read `/v2/agents/{agent_id}/runs` instead of a path with an empty segment.
- The run header's "Raise the cap" no longer shows on a run that waits on you for something other than its cap: an approval, a question, its browser or its computer (its served `pending`). A run paused at its cap still offers it, and so does a capped run that is still working.
- The console never shows the password's sign-in when it can't read the server's session. Its first read of `GET /v2/auth/session` decides between setup, sign-in and the console; a read that failed (a proxy's page answering in the server's place, a refusal, the connection cut) was taken as "this server has a password" and showed Sign in, so a fresh server with no owner could open on a sign-in it can't take. This is what a fresh Render server's address did for its first minute or so: its edge answers 502 with its own page until the server is up. Now a gateway's 502, 503 or 504 page says the server is starting, and any other failure says the page can't read the server's sign-in, with what answered. Either way the page asks again on its own (1 s after, doubling to 30 s, with no end) and with Retry now, and opens setup or sign-in once the server answers.
- A template's Apply form names a refused parameter by the label it shows ("risk_appetite is required."), never its raw path ("parameters.risk_appetite …"), in words from the served issue's reason. An issue goes as soon as its field's value is valid, and the refusal beside Apply goes with the last of them. Issues are placed by the served `field`.
- The console's window no longer scrolls away from its sidebar. On Settings → Keys the whole page could scroll past the window, leaving the sidebar cut short over blank space: text kept for a screen reader alone was placed in the document and stretched it. The console's outer box and its page box now hold what is positioned inside them, on every page, so the only scrollbar is the page's own.

## 0.1.0-beta.32 (2026-11-10)

- Everything the agents make is now on the disk, apart from the data directory's own files. Each agent has one folder, `<data>/disk/agents/<agent>/` (its id in lower case, a dash, and 16 hex digits of its SHA-256). That folder holds the agent's learning folder (`learning/`) and its runs (`runs/<run>/`, each with `store/run.sqlite` and `work/`). store.sqlite and the server's files stay at the top of the data directory, as the control plane, and only the server opens them. A run's row in store.sqlite now holds its claim: `claim_epoch` and `claim_holder`. Each launch of the run's process bumps the epoch, with that process as the holder, and hands the process its epoch. Once the process is gone, the server takes the run back: it bumps the epoch, names itself the holder, and checkpoints the run's database. A run's database (pi-durable-disk's exclusive profile) is WAL, under an exclusive lock, with `wal_autocheckpoint = 0` and synchronous FULL. The run's process ends with its WAL in place: its store's close keeps the connection open (`keepWal`), and the process ends by process.exit, which never checkpoints. A test pins this node:sqlite behaviour, and its inverse: a close does checkpoint. The server reads a run's database with a fresh read-only, lock-free open (`vfs=unix-none`, EXCLUSIVE) per read. It opens the WAL only once the WAL holds a commit that SQLite's recovery would take: a frame with the header's salts, a valid chained checksum and a nonzero commit size. Until then it reads the database file alone, as an immutable file. A lock-free reader of a WAL whose first transaction is still being written recovers no frame, and its close removes that WAL, which loses the writer's rows. agentrun-pi exports that check as `walHasCommit`, and both readers use it. It never shows a read whose last entry is behind one it read before. It never opens the database for writing while a process holds the run. A data directory an earlier version wrote (runs under `<data>/runs/`, or a store of store version 1) is refused at start, in words, and nothing is migrated. pi-durable-agent's store is now store version 2. It names a run's folder by its row's agent (`runDir`, `runStorePath`), and adds `claimRun` and `runClaim`. A store of version 1 is refused. serve gains the local disk adapter: `mount`, `sync`, `unmount` and `revoke` for one machine, where a runner works in the folder itself and a revoke is the server's take-back of the claim. agentrun-pi's durable readers take `unlocked` (a fresh `vfs=unix-none` read, as the server's), which serve's reads of a run's decisions use. A run's folder is never made through a symbolic link: a link on the way to it refuses the run's start, and refuses an import that finishes a run its last import left without a transcript (pi-durable-agent `runFolders`).
- The E2B provider takes no `publicStream` option and has no `stream()`: a screen is streamed through agentrun, never through a vendor URL. `publicStream` let public traffic into the sandbox, which opened every port in the computer to anyone with its address. Making an `E2BProvider` with `publicStream` (either value) now throws in words, and the conformance module no longer reads `COMPUTER_E2B_PUBLIC_STREAM`. Drop the option and the setting; every E2B sandbox is made with public traffic off. `provider.stream` stays on `ComputerProvider` for the browser kinds (Browserbase and Kernel).
- Remote computers (Daytona, E2B, Modal) cut the daemon's programs off from the vendor's own agent in the sandbox (Daytona's toolbox on 2280, E2B's envd on 49983), which runs commands as root or with sudo for whoever reaches it. Each starts `computerd init --user <uid:gid> --own-network`. Init, still root, does four things: it refuses uid 0 for its programs; it refuses to start while another process holds their uid; it moves into a network namespace that holds its loopback alone; and once dropped, it refuses AF_VSOCK sockets and io_uring_setup on every thread. Without CAP_SYS_ADMIN and CAP_NET_ADMIN, init refuses to start in words and the computer fails closed.

  Who breaks, and what to do:
  - A Daytona, E2B or Modal spec with network `open` or an allowlist is refused in words by `check()` and `create()`, and `network()` refuses a change to one. Ask for `none`. A proxy-backed open network on remote computers is the next feature after this one.
  - The default computer user on Daytona, E2B and Modal is 2510:2510 (was 1000:1000). The `daemonUser` and `user` options still name another one.
  - `capabilities().vendorAgentPorts` declares the ports of a vendor's agent: Daytona `[2280]`, E2B `[49983]`, Modal `[]`. The new Core conformance case `isolation.vendor-agent` probes them on both loopbacks as the computer's user, along with `1.1.1.1:443` and `example.com:443`, and requires every target refused, with the loopback alone in view. A provider with daemon `child` must declare the ports.
  - `computerd probe` reports `interfaces`. The Daytona and E2B drift checks require the daemon's network to hold `lo` alone and its seccomp mode to be 2. Modal's probe requires `interfaces` to read `["lo"]`.
- The computer library reads no environment: every provider is built by its file's `create(options)`, and every setting, a vendor key included, is an option. `ENV`, `fromEnv`, Daytona's `optionsFromEnv`, Modal's `modalServerUrl` and the registry's `providerFromEnv` are gone; `providerFor(id, options)` builds a provider by id. Daytona's API URL (default Daytona's own) and target are `apiUrl` and `target`; Modal's token, app and server are `tokenId`, `tokenSecret`, `app` (required, refused in words naming the option) and `serverUrl`; E2B's key is `apiKey`. Serve passes its own existing names at the cutover (`DAYTONA_API_KEY`, `DAYTONA_API_URL`, `DAYTONA_TARGET`, `MODAL_TOKEN_ID`, `MODAL_TOKEN_SECRET`, `AGENTRUN_BOX_APP`, `AGENTRUN_DOCKER_IMAGE`, and `E2B_API_KEY` as its new one), so an owner's settings keep working. Only `src/conformance/` (the conformance CLI, each provider's conformance module and the sweeps) reads the `COMPUTER_*` names, through each module's `settingsFromEnv(env)`, so the live scripts run unchanged. A default never comes from the environment either: Docker's `daemonCache` defaults to `~/.cache/agentrun-computer` and no longer reads `XDG_CACHE_HOME` (pass `daemonCache` to keep the copy elsewhere; the conformance CLI reads `COMPUTER_DOCKER_DAEMON_CACHE`). Two guards stay, each against a vendor SDK that reads the environment on its own: Modal's provider refuses an ambient `MODAL_SERVER_URL` and sets it only around the client's construction, now one construction at a time and put back after each; E2B's refuses an ambient `E2B_SANDBOX_URL`. `scripts/check-env.mjs` reads `src/` with TypeScript's parser and fails on any other use of `process.env` outside `src/conformance/`.

  A Docker computer whose lifetime ran out before `docker run` finished (a Docker host that held the start; init found its deadline past and exited 0) now fails `create` as `timeout`, "its lifetime of N s ended before docker run finished (it took M s)", where it was a `docker` failure saying only that the computer was not running after its start. The conformance case `provider.deadline` reads the run's clock. On a host slower to start a computer than the case's 45 s lifetime, it asks once more for 45 s past twice what create took. When that is still too slow it skips in words naming both times, so Core does not pass.
- The computer protocol extension's types and conformance cases: a computer has a kind, a daemon mode and groups, and the suite's Core depends on them.
  - `capabilities()` gains `kind` (`linux`, `desktop`, `android`, `ios`), `groups` (`exec`, `files`, `processes`, `screen`, `input.pointer`, `input.keys`, `input.touch`, `apps`, `apps.install`, `apps.ui_tree`) and `maxLifetimeMs`, and `daemon` becomes a mode (`pid1`, `child` or `none`) instead of a list. Hello reports `kind` and `groups` in place of `capabilities`; the drift check and Docker's `connect` refuse a hello whose kind or groups differ, in either direction. Docker declares `linux`, `pid1`, exec, files and processes, and a 30-day cap. Code that read `capabilities().daemon` or `hello.capabilities` reads `groups`; the `DaemonCapability` type is removed (use `Group`).
  - `protocol.ts` has the screen, input and apps methods' types, `KEYS`, `UiNode`, `ComputerClient` (which `DaemonClient` implements) and a typed `deadline.extend`; `checkActions()` is the batch check of `input.actions`. `checkLifetime()` is one lifetime rule for every provider's `check()`, `kindRefusals()` names an invalid kind, os and daemon, and a provider may offer `extend()` (an earlier deadline, or one past create + `maxLifetimeMs`, is refused with the deadline unchanged) and `stream()`. The janitor deletes a computer whose deadline is more than `maxLifetimeMs` past now.
  - `coreGroups(kind, daemon)` replaces `CORE_CAPABILITIES`. A Core case missing what its kind requires now fails in words instead of skipping; one missing an optional group skips, is named in `undeclared`, and is left out of Core's count. Thirteen new cases (`health.kind-consistent`, and the `screen`, `input`, `touch`, `apps` and `lifetime` groups) check effects against a fixture's event log, named with `--fixture`; on Docker the twelve that need a group or method it lacks skip in words, and Core reads `53/53`. `readPng()` decodes a whole 8-bit, non-interlaced PNG when it reads it, bounded by its IHDR. The report names the kind and daemon mode. The conformance `Group` type is now `CaseGroup`; `Group` is the protocol's.
- `@parcha/pi-agent-learn` and `@parcha/pi-git-remote` are deleted from the repository, with the `examples/agent-learn` demo (`npm run demo:learn`) and the guide's "An agent that learns" section. No other package imports either one, so nothing else changes. Neither is published again: their last versions on npm stay installable and are deprecated there. A program that uses them keeps working on the last published version; a server's agent learning (agentrun-serve's learning folder) is unaffected.
- `@parcha/pi-vision` is gone from this repository, with the reference host it served. No package of the open platform imported it. It gets no new release, and its versions on npm are deprecated. The same change removes:
  - `@parcha/agentrun-harness`, the reference host for the demos. It was held and never published.
  - `apps/viewer`, the recording player, and the GitHub Pages gallery that published it.
  - the examples that ran on the reference host or on pi-vision: `examples/reference`, `hero`, `issue-triage`, `vision-agent`, `web` and the vision question folders.
  - the `demo:reference`, `demo:kill`, `demo:hero`, `demo:issue-triage`, `demo:vision` and `test:viewer` scripts.

  A program that imports `@parcha/pi-vision` can keep the last published version, which stays installable.
- A workflow run's graph, once the run has ended, and the server's reads at the run's end (`workflowCarry` and `readWorkflowRun` of its outcome) now read the run's database lock-free, as every other server read does. They make no -wal or -shm file beside it, never wait on a lock, and never remove a WAL that holds commits. `GET /runs/{run_id}/graph` drops its `stale` field. The view it marked (the last whole view, or the live view, shown while the run's store was busy) can no longer happen, since a lock-free read never meets a busy store. The console no longer reads the field. pi-durable-agent's `readWorkflowRun(run, { unlocked })` and `workflowCarry(run, outcome, { unlocked })` take the unlocked read. agentrun-pi exports `withDurableFile`.
- The pi family is 1.5.0 exactly (pi-durable, pi-ai, pi-coding-agent, chord), with one typebox (1.3.27); the older-pi CI legs are gone. A pi 1.5 run store is SQLite schema 2, which pi 1.3.0 or earlier cannot open again, so there is no rollback past this release. Tool results are under `output` only. A browser charge serve fails to count makes the run's spend unknown, and a capped run then refuses its next paid call until the spend is known again. The door's `GET /events` and a recording's live lines read through an adapter on pi 1.5's `observeCommits` (pi-durable-disk `./internals` `watchEvents`, with no compatibility promise) until they move to `conversation.watch`. A failed run store's close releases this process's live browser sessions again; a normal close keeps them for the next run. A claude-agent, codex or opencode run opens on pi 1.5, and an image it reads through pi's read tool is read. particle-1's cap hold keeps Particle's list price over pi-ai's promotional catalog price; a request is still booked at OpenRouter's billed total. The agent store reader reads pi-durable schema 2.
- An agent can run unattended. Its definition takes `approvals`: `ask`, the default, is the tool gate as its settings say; `never` allows each tool that would ask instead, decided by the gate at each call, so a tool granted later and an MCP tool (`mcp:<server>/<tool>`, which asks by default) are covered on every harness. It never lifts a deny, whether set on the server or in the agent's own `tool_settings`, nor anything else the gate refuses before it decides between running and asking. A workflow's call node on a tool set to ask runs under `never`. The agent read serves `approvals`, its tools say an allow came from `unattended` (`permission_from`), and the run read serves the run's own `approvals`, as it started. A template apply's `overrides.approvals` sets it; an apply without it takes the package's own default (`approvals_default`: `never` for a template whose job runs on triggers, else `ask`). A value other than `ask` or `never` is a `422` naming `definition.approvals`. Upgrading: nothing to do; an agent that names none asks, as before.
- `@parcha/agentrun` ships an Agent Skills folder, `skills/agentrun-cli`, so a coding agent can drive the server through the CLI. Its `SKILL.md` covers how the CLI finds the server and the token, five recipes as shell blocks (make an agent from a definition file, start a capped run and wait, answer a run that asks, read what it wrote and what it cost, raise a cap or approve a call), the exit codes and one line per command group. `references/commands.md` has one entry per command with its usage, summary, flags and route. Both are generated with the command table by `node scripts/generate-clients.mjs`, and their header holds the table's hash. `--check` fails when either differs from the generated files, or when the skill names a command or flag the CLI does not have. Recipe 5 stops a run at a cap too small for it (exit 8), raises the cap with `agentrun runs raise-cap RUN_ID --max-cost-usd N` and waits for the run to complete. The generator picks that command from the table by its operation. A tool call's approval is answered by the person in the console, since the server takes that answer from no token, and the recipe says so.
  `agentrun skill install [--claude] [--codex] [--dir DIR]` copies the skill to `~/.claude/skills/agentrun-cli` and `~/.agents/skills/agentrun-cli` (the shared folder Codex reads), by default to each of the two whose folder exists, or to `DIR/agentrun-cli`. It prints the paths it wrote. It refuses (exit 2, nothing touched) a target that is the package's own skill folder, through a link too, lies inside it or holds it, and it replaces an older copy of the skill by copying beside it and renaming, so a failed copy leaves the old one in place.
  `agentrun tool-keys set KEY_ID FIELD [--from-env VAR]` saves one field of a tool key. The value is read from stdin or from the named environment variable, never from the command line, and appears in no output or error. A `KEY_ID` that is empty, `.` or `..` is refused, and the request is sent only when its URL, the base URL's path included, is the tool key route.
  `agentrun login [--base-url URL]` reads a token from stdin and refuses one typed on a terminal. It checks the token against the server (a refused token is exit 4 and nothing is written), then writes `base_url` and `api_key` to `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/agentrun/config.json` (else `~/.config/agentrun/config.json`) with mode 0600 in a 0700 directory. `agentrun logout` removes the file. A token that is not a valid header value is refused before anything is sent, exit 4.
  Every command refuses a base URL that is not http or https or has a query, a fragment, a user or a password (exit 2), from `--base-url`, `AGENTRUN_BASE_URL` or the config file: such a URL could swallow the path appended to it, so another route answered. `login` saves the token only when the check's answer is the agents list.
  A command that sends a secret (`login`, `tool-keys set`, `tool-keys save`, `mcp-servers save|preview`, and `api` to their routes) never prints the server's error body: a refusal prints its status, its `error.code` and a message of the CLI's own, with the same exit code as before. As a second line against a server that echoes what it was sent, a secret's copies in what it does print, as it is, JSON-escaped or URL-encoded, are cut out: from a JSON answer's string values and keys, so the answer printed is still JSON with its structure intact, and from the text of any other answer. Every string value of such a body is a secret except the public fields `scripts/clients/resources.json` names by full path (an MCP server's `url` and `auth.type`, a preview's `name`), which `generate-clients --check` holds to the body's schema. A header's value is always a secret, whatever the header is called.
  An MCP server's OAuth sign-in has `agentrun mcp-servers oauth-start NAME`, its body (with any client secret) from stdin or a file, and printing the page to open with its URL, scopes and client id intact, and `mcp-servers oauth-client` reads this server's client metadata document. The callback, which only the owner's browser reaches, has no command.
  The `agentrun-serve` skill and the server's `llms.txt` point to the CLI skill for everything the MCP tools do not cover.
- `npx @parcha/agentrun runs wait RUN_ID` waits until a run ends or waits on a person and prints it, and `runs start ... --wait` starts a run and does the same: exit 0 completed, 1 failed or cancelled, 8 it waits on a person (a question, its spend cap, a stopped turn, or a paused workflow run), and the line on stderr says what to do next. It long-polls the run's timeline, so it answers as soon as the run changes. `--timeout SECONDS` stops waiting after that long, prints the run as it is and exits 1 with a line saying how to wait again; the run goes on.
  `runs timeline RUN_ID --follow` streams the timeline as it changes over the route's event stream, one page per line (NDJSON) as the server sends it: its rows, the ids it drops and `reset` when the rows held are to go first. A dropped connection resumes with `Last-Event-ID`, so no change comes twice, and the command ends when the run ends: 0 completed, 1 failed or cancelled.
- `@parcha/agentrun-client` is published from this release on: `npm install @parcha/agentrun-client` installs the typed TypeScript client for a self-hosted AgentRun server, at the release's version, with no dependencies. The release checks it as it checks every published package (its manifest, its packed files, and a clean install that imports it and calls a fake server with no network).
- `DaytonaProvider`, `ModalProvider` and `E2BProvider` mount the run's folder from an Archil disk: each declares `mount: ["archil"]` (`MOUNT`). For a spec with `mount: { kind: "archil", disk, region, folder, token }`, `create` runs the shared Archil steps (preflight, the takeover of the run's lower epochs, mount or revoke with the token on the helper's stdin, serve) as root through the vendor's own exec, after the daemon is in place and before init starts, then reads the mount back from inside. The token reaches the helper's stdin alone: never an argument, a label, a tag, metadata, a read-back or an error.
  - `MountReadBack`'s archil arm gains `exclusive` and `epoch` (the computer's `agentrun.epoch` label). `readBackOf(mount, epoch?)` keeps the epoch optional; an Archil mount with none reads back as `{ other }`, in words.
  - `box/` ships the root helper `agentrun-archil` (mount, revoke, serve, checkin; root only, never through sudo) and the image recipe: archil 0.10.1 pinned by sha256, the tool user `agentrun-tool` 2510:2510, no sudo and no sudoers file, the image's user root. A mount gives the folder's top to the tool user right after it, whatever owner the disk's object API made it with. The image installs no firewall: init and every tool run in a network namespace of their own.
  - computerd gains `sync-serve` (the helper's serve: a resident sync on /run/agentrun-archil/archil.sock, folder 0750 and socket 0660, root's and group 2510; one fixed request byte runs `archil sync`, coalesced, and answers its exit code; it holds no token and exits when the mount goes or its socket is removed) and the `mount.sync` call, which trusts only a root-owned 0750 folder of the tool user's group and a root-owned socket.
  - An Archil spec takes network `"none"` and the run's `agentrun.run` and `agentrun.epoch` labels, its folder `agents/<agent>/runs/<run>/work` of that run. Its tools run as `agentrun-tool`, 2510:2510: Daytona and E2B take an Archil spec only from a provider made with `daemonUser: "2510:2510"`; Modal makes that an Archil spec's default user and refuses any other; E2B refuses a spec label named `agentrun.mount`.
  - The vendor network stays on, for root to reach Archil: Daytona's network is open, Modal drops `blockNetwork`, and E2B keeps internet access on with public traffic off. The tools' network `"none"` is init's own.
  - Daytona: each root step is a toolbox session command of its own, the token sent as its input with no echo; the label `agentrun.mount` records the mount's kind and source.
  - Modal: the sandbox's command is always the entrypoint, which waits for a root-made gate (`/opt/agentrun/archil-mounted`) as well as the daemon, so no tool runs before the folder is mounted. The spec tag records the mount as it reads back, under the claim's epoch. `ModalTimeouts` and `E2BTimeouts` gain `mountMs`, the bound of each root step.
  - E2B: each root step is a background root command with stdin (`sendStdin`, then `closeStdin`); the metadata `agentrun.mount` records the mount's kind and source.
  - `connect()` on an Archil computer syncs the folder after every call that may change it (`process.start` included, whose log may be made in `/workspace`), coalesced; a failed sync fails the call, and a caller whose signal fires stops waiting for the shared sync, answered `aborted`. A create never forces off another computer of the run at its own epoch: a held folder then refuses in words. `release` lets the folder go (a final sync, then checkin) while the computer runs, then deletes it, and succeeds once it is gone: a folder not let go cleanly loses nothing, so it is said through the provider's new `onNote` option, never as a failed release. `list()` reports the recorded mount under the epoch label; `attach` reads it back from inside and refuses drift.
  - The conformance suite: `src/conformance/archil.ts` gives every remote provider's module one Archil run folder (`archilRunFolder`, `archilSettings`, `ARCHIL_ENV`), reached from outside through the disk's object API (the host's `disk` SDK, or one given). The Daytona, Modal and E2B modules export `mountFor("archil")`, reading `COMPUTER_ARCHIL_DISK`, `COMPUTER_ARCHIL_REGION`, `COMPUTER_ARCHIL_TOKEN`, `COMPUTER_ARCHIL_API_KEY` and `COMPUTER_ARCHIL_S3_URL`. New cases: mount.archil-durable, mount.archil-takeover, mount.archil-token and mount.archil-isolated-durable; a case may record what it saw without asserting it (`CaseResult.observed`), and the CLI puts what a provider says through `onNote` in the report's `providerNotes`. An Archil computer's `network()` reads `"none"` from the daemon, its own loopback alone, since its vendor network is open for root.
- Browserbase and Kernel are computers of kind `browser` (`src/browserbase.ts`, `src/kernel.ts`), registered in `providers.ts` and held to their `PROVIDERS` entries. Their `CAPABILITIES` are `cdp` and `stream`.

  - Each is built by `create(options)` from `apiKey` (Browserbase also takes `projectId`) or from a host's own transport (`api`, `request`). Each calls the vendor's REST API at the same endpoints pi-browser calls today.
  - Labels ride the vendor session's metadata. `attach` finds a session by id or by labels, `list` by labels. `connect` reads the browser first and gives `cdp.url`, the signed CDP URL, which is a secret. A browser that has ended, or that the vendor no longer knows though its URL is held, is `gone`. `stream` gives the live view. `release` is idempotent and confirmed by a read-back. `check()` refuses a lifetime past the vendor's cap. Neither has `extend`.
  - Each create also writes the label `agentrun.create` with a value of its own. A create whose reply is lost, or whose caller cancels it, finds the browser it made by that label and releases it, and never creates again. `check()` refuses the label in a spec, and list and attach never show it.
  - `testConnection()` makes one read of the account's running sessions or browsers and never creates one.
  - The conformance CLI runs the browser case set (health, then `browser.*`) for a provider of kind browser. The set shares one session per run, and `--kind browser` passes on it. The conformance modules read `COMPUTER_BROWSERBASE_*` and `COMPUTER_KERNEL_*` behind a live switch.
  - Browserbase refuses a dot in a userMetadata key, as found live. A label's dots are written there as `_` and read back as dots, and `check()` takes Browserbase label keys of letters, digits and dots only, with no `'` or `\` in a value.
- A conformance suite for computer providers: `npm run conformance -w @parcha/agentrun-computer -- --provider docker` (or `--provider ./module.js`, a module that exports `createProvider()`) runs every case against the provider, prints one line per level and group with each failure's reason, and exits 0 when Core passes, 1 when it fails and 2 on a usage error; `--json` prints the report.
  - Each case is data: an id, a level (`core` or `full`, after the Kubernetes agent-sandbox conformance levels), a group (`health`, `exec`, `files`, `process`, `provider`, `drift`) and the capabilities it needs. A case whose needs the provider or its daemon does not declare is skipped with the reason, never failed; Core passes only when every Core case passes.
  - Cases assume no root: a computer's programs run as the provider's non-root default with no capabilities, and the suite works under the computer's `HOME`. `capabilities().user` (`["root", "uid"]` for Docker) declares which `spec.user` requests a provider takes; the Full cases `exec.root-on-request` and `exec.user-uid` need `user:root` and `user:uid`.
  - Core needs network `none`: the suite never asks for a wider network, so a provider without it fails Core in words. Every provider call the runner makes is bounded, so a stalled provider gives a failed report and the CLI exits; a computer a provider gives after its case ended is deleted as it arrives and named in `cleanup.late`.
  - `runConformance(provider, { image, only?, signal?, drifted? })` and `formatReport(report)` run it from code. The run labels every computer it makes, deletes each one, sweeps its label when the provider's lifetime is `"janitor"`, and reports any computer left.
  - `npm run test:docker` runs the suite on Docker, one test per case, and the CLI against `alpine:3.20`; the Docker live tests keep only what Docker alone shows.
- `DaytonaProvider`, the second computer provider, over Daytona's v0 REST API with `fetch` and no new dependency. A snapshot is the computer's image; `image: "default"` sends none, so Daytona uses its default snapshot. Capabilities: kind `linux`, daemon `child` (init is a session command of Daytona's toolbox), groups `exec`, `files` and `processes`, lifetime `vendor` (the sandbox's TTL), `maxLifetimeMs` 30 days, network `none`, `open` or one allowlist (up to 100 domains on ports 80 and 443, or 10 IPv4 CIDRs), and `release` `["stop", "delete"]`. `create` takes `autoStopMinutes` and `autoDeleteMinutes`, pushes `computerd` in through the toolbox, installs it root-owned at a path no other user can change, and starts `computerd init --user 1000:1000` as root; init drops itself to 1000:1000 with no groups, no capabilities and `no_new_privs` on every thread. `connect` runs `computerd stdio` in a session of its own; init and the bridge refuse a daemon whose path a user other than root could have changed. The drift check reads the sandbox and the daemon back at create and attach. `release` waits until Daytona reads the computer gone or stopped. Four live runs reached the default snapshot; the last passed Core 53/53.
  - The sandbox is the only boundary: a program in a Daytona computer can become root through Daytona's toolbox, which listens on port 2280 with no authentication and runs commands as a user with passwordless `sudo` in the default snapshot. The daemon's user is hygiene, and `networkBlockAll` limits egress only.
  - `dist/conformance/daytona.js` runs the conformance suite on Daytona, reading `COMPUTER_DAYTONA_API_KEY` and the other `COMPUTER_DAYTONA_*` settings by name, never `DAYTONA_API_KEY`; its computers stop after 30 idle minutes and a stop deletes. `dist/conformance/daytona-sweep.js --before <file>` and `--after <file>` delete the labelled sandboxes a run left behind.
  - `ComputerCapabilities` gains `stopDeletes` (a stop ends in deletion; Core `provider.stop` holds a provider to it, and Full `provider.janitor` skips) and `allow`; `ReadBackSpec` gains `snapshot`; results gain a `daytona` error code. The conformance CLI takes a module's `conformanceImage()` when `--image` names none and prints its `conformanceNotes`.
- `PROVIDERS` entries carry `defaultImage`: the image a computer of each setting kind starts from when its setting names none, so serve no longer keeps its own table. Each value is the constant its provider module owns: `DAYTONA_DEFAULT_SNAPSHOT` and `BROWSER_IMAGE` as before, and new named constants in the E2B, Orgo, Modal and phone modules where only a literal stood (their refusal words and conformance images now use them too). A kind with no entry has no default, and the caller refuses the setting in words. Docker has none, since serve builds its image.
- A Docker computer takes network `{ allow: [names or CIDRs] }` beside `none` and `open`: `capabilities().network` is `["none", "open", "allow"]`, and an allowlist holds exact names, `*.suffix` names and CIDRs (host bits zero, over no range the address rule refuses), at most 256 of each, on ports 80 and 443. The computer itself stays `--network none`. Its one way out is its own egress proxy, serve's proxy and address table moved into `src/docker/egress-proxy.ts` and `src/docker/egress-table.ts`, which runs in a second container the provider makes, reads back and removes with it.
  - `create` makes a tmpfs volume (`computer-<hex>-egress`, mode 0700, the computer's uid:gid), starts the proxy container on Docker's default bridge alone (no port, the computer's user, a read-only root, no capability, `no-new-privileges`, bounded memory, pids, CPU (half of one), open files and log; `EGRESS_IMAGE`, node:22.23.4-bookworm-slim pinned by digest, or `egress.image`), and waits for its ready line (`egress.readyMs`, 10 s) before it runs the computer, which mounts the volume read-only at `/opt/agentrun/egress`. A proxy that ends first, or is not ready in time, fails create in words, and the proxy and the volume are removed. On a host that remaps user ids (userns-remap, rootless Docker) `check` and `create` refuse an allowlist in words before anything is made, as they do a bind. So they do when this provider's interfaces are not the Docker host's (it runs in a container, or `docker context inspect` names a daemon that is not a local unix socket), unless `egress.hostAddresses` names the host's addresses, which the proxy then refuses as `this-host`. Before it makes anything, `create` makes sure the computer's image and the proxy's are on the Docker host (`docker image inspect`, then `docker pull` for one that is not; a failed pull fails create in words naming the image), and both containers run with `--pull never`, so no pull happens while the sweep could take the new proxy for a leftover.
  - computerd `init --egress <socket>` relays its loopback port 3128 to the proxy's socket and gives every program it starts `HTTP_PROXY`, `HTTPS_PROXY`, their lowercase forms and `NO_PROXY`. A program that ignores them reaches nothing. Init refuses to start when it cannot listen.
  - The proxy refuses, before any socket opens, a name off the list (before any lookup), an IP literal outside a listed CIDR, any port but 80 and 443, and a listed name or address that is private, loopback, link-local, metadata or this machine's own, in any form; it resolves a name once and dials the checked address, never follows a redirect, refuses SOCKS5 BIND and UDP ASSOCIATE, and logs no path, query or header. It ends itself at the computer's deadline.
  - `network`, `list` and `attach` read the allowlist back from Docker: the computer's network mode, its egress volume and relay, and the proxy container's label, deadline, state, hardening, bounds (memory, swap, pids, CPU quota, open files, IPC and cgroup namespaces, log), mounts and argv, whose `--local` must hold every one of the Docker host's own addresses the provider requires now (this machine's public ones and `egress.hostAddresses`; a host whose addresses changed since reads as other). Anything less, a crashed proxy included, reads back `{ other }` in words, and `attach` refuses it. `network(ref, policy)` returns an allowlist the computer has, in any order; every change is refused, since a Docker computer's network is fixed at create.
  - `release` stops or removes the computer's proxy and, on delete, its volume, each found by the label `agentrun.computer.egress=<computer>`, so the janitor's sweep and `releaseOlder` remove them too. Parts left with no computer (one removed by hand, or a provider that died between its proxy and its computer) the sweep removes through the new optional `ComputerProvider.sweepLeftovers`: a proxy or volume with the egress label whose deadline has passed, or whose computer is gone past a create's grace; `Swept.leftovers` names them. The conformance cases `provider.network-allow` and `provider.network-switch-allow` now run and pass on Docker.
- The Docker provider owns the image its computers start from: `ensureImage(source, { docker?, signal?, timeoutMs?, onWait? })` (`src/docker/image.ts`) and `DockerProvider`'s `image` option and `image()` method make it ready in the provider's own process and give its tag for `create`. There is no state file between processes: Docker's own tag is the shared state.
  - `{ recipe }`, a Dockerfile with no build context, is built with `docker build -` (the recipe on stdin), tagged `agentrun-computer:<first 12 hex digits of its sha256>` and labelled `agentrun.computer.recipe=<the whole sha256>`, and only when Docker has no image of that recipe under the tag. `{ image }`, an image by name, is pulled once when Docker has none, and never built.
  - The default is the provider's own recipe, `DOCKER_RECIPE` (`DEFAULT_DOCKER_IMAGE`): `python:3.12-slim` pinned by its index digest, with git, curl, nodejs, npm, ripgrep and socat. Another image is named only with the `image` option (to `new DockerProvider` or to `fromEnv`'s options): there is no env name for it, and `fromEnv` reads no image name from the environment. serve keeps reading `AGENTRUN_DOCKER_IMAGE` and passes it in as the `image` option at the cutover.
  - Concurrent callers in one process share one build; a failure is never kept, so the next call tries again. Two processes that miss the tag at once both build the same recipe; a build that then fails (Docker 29's image store refuses all but the first tag with `already exists`) asks Docker once more, so both end with the tag on an image of it. `onWait(making, tag)` is told once before a caller waits on a build or pull, never when Docker has the image.
  - One bound, `timeoutMs` (default 10 minutes), covers the inspect and the build or pull; past it the docker CLI is killed. A caller's `signal` ends its own wait, and the last caller's abort stops the build.
  - Failures are in words with none of Docker's output: Docker not answering, a build that could not reach the network, pull its base, find disk space or install a package, a recipe Docker could not parse at a line or whose `RUN` step failed with an exit code, and a pull the registry refused, does not have, or could not be reached for.
  - The live desktop suite builds its fixture image through `ensureImage`.
- An E2B provider, one registry line (`e2b`): `E2BProvider` in `dist/e2b.js` makes E2B sandboxes from a template, `desktop` (E2B Desktop) by default, as kind `desktop`, or kind `linux` with `COMPUTER_E2B_KIND=linux`. computerd is a `child` of E2B's envd: create uploads it as root, checks its sha256, and starts `computerd init` as a background command with no command timeout, `DISPLAY` and `--max-deadline`; the X server starts through the daemon, as on a Docker desktop, and hello re-checks the display on every connect. Every call goes through the host's `e2b` package (2.63.0), of which the provider names only the slice it calls and imports nothing at load.
  - The key is `COMPUTER_E2B_API_KEY` alone, never `E2B_API_KEY`: every SDK call is given the key, the domain (`COMPUTER_E2B_DOMAIN`), the API URL and `debug: false` explicitly, so no ambient `E2B_*` is read, and the provider refuses to start while `E2B_SANDBOX_URL` is set, which no option clears.
  - Lifetime: E2B's timeout is the lifetime, capped at Hobby's 1 h by default and up to Pro's 24 h with `COMPUTER_E2B_MAX_LIFETIME_MS`; `extend()` moves E2B's timer, then init's deadline, never past create's time plus the cap.
  - Network none is no internet access and no public traffic, read back by `getInfo` and probed from inside under the shared rule (only an answer refuses it). E2B's firewall accepts each outbound TCP connection and then closes a denied one with nothing sent back, so a computer with network none is kept, and create's and attach's `notes` name the accepted targets. The probe's defaults are Modal's: TCP to 443, and DNS over UDP to `1.1.1.1:53/udp` and `[2606:4700:4700::1111]:53/udp`; E2B drops non-TCP to the deny set in nftables, so a UDP target gets no reply within the 10 s window and reads refused, and a reply refuses network none. `probeTargets` takes `host:port/udp`. That rule holds for every provider (confirmed 11-07), and its residual risk is a one-way leak no probe from inside can see. Open is internet access, still with no public traffic. `stream()` exists only with `COMPUTER_E2B_PUBLIC_STREAM=1`, whose computers are network open: x11vnc on loopback with an 8-character password and noVNC in front, the URL never logged.
  - Release kills and waits until E2B no longer has or lists the sandbox, within `goneMs` and the caller's signal; a create that fails or is aborted at any step kills its sandbox. `dist/conformance/e2b.js` (live switch `AGENTRUN_COMPUTER_LIVE_E2B=1`, 30-minute computers) and `dist/conformance/e2b-sweep.js` come with it.
  - The child daemon's install, init and stdio commands and its read-back move from `daytona.ts` to `child.ts`, and the display start from `docker.ts` to `desktop.ts`, shared; Daytona's commands are unchanged.
  - `computerd probe --diagnose` reports, per public address and port (1.1.1.1, 8.8.8.8, 9.9.9.9 and Fastly's 151.101.1.57 on 443/tcp, 80/tcp and 53/udp), whether the connection was refused, accepted with no answer, or answered (any byte back, judged as `--connect` judges a target), every step bounded. `dist/conformance/e2b-diagnose.js` runs it in one sandbox made as create makes one for network none, since E2B's firewall accepts a TCP connection before it decides, and always kills the sandbox and reads it back gone. It records the provider's own check, `passed` with its `notes` or its refusal.
- Every computer of a run carries two labels, `agentrun.run` and `agentrun.epoch` (`RUN_LABEL`, `EPOCH_LABEL`, in `src/labels.ts`), and a takeover releases the run's computers of an older claim with `releaseOlder(provider, run, epoch, { signal? })`. The claim epoch is the one fence for runs and computers: a new claim is epoch + 1, and its first step deletes every computer of the run labelled with a lower epoch.
  - `runLabels(run, epoch)` gives the two labels; a run id is a non-empty string with no NUL byte (`runProblem`), an epoch a whole number from 0 to 2^53 - 1 (`epochProblem`). `epochOf(labels)` reads a computer's epoch, decimal with no sign, leading zero or space, and names anything else in words. No provider refuses the labels: they are outside its own `agentrun.computer` namespace, and Docker takes them with no code of its own.
  - `releaseOlder` lists by `agentrun.run`, holds each listed computer to the run's label again, deletes each with a lower epoch or with no epoch it can read (named in `unproven`), keeps this epoch's, and returns `{ released, kept, unproven }`. A computer of the run with a higher epoch refuses the call as fenced before anything is released. Every release is attempted, and when any fails the result is an error naming each computer left and why: a partial cleanup is never a success. An aborted call starts no further release and says which ran. It revokes no mount; there is no `unmount` on the provider.
  - The conformance suite has a Full case, `provider.release-older`: computers of one run at epochs 1, 2 and 3 and of another run at epoch 1; `releaseOlder` at epoch 3 leaves 3 and the other run's, and at epoch 2 after it is refused as fenced.
- Files move in and out of a computer through its daemon: `files.read`, `files.write`, `files.stat`, `files.list`, `files.mkdir`, `files.move` and `files.remove` on absolute paths (a relative one is `invalid`), and tar archives both ways without holding one in memory: `tar.pull.begin`, `tar.pull.read` (at most 512 KiB a read) and `tar.pull.end` read an archive as it is written, and `tar.push.begin`, `tar.push.write` and `tar.push.end` extract into a directory as the bytes come. Hello's capabilities gain `files`.
  - A pushed entry that is absolute, has a `..` part, or would be written through a symlink out of the target directory is refused as `denied`; modes are kept (a directory's once the archive ends) and nothing is chowned; `files.move` without `overwrite` never replaces, even under a concurrent move; a push still open when the connection ends is ended, leaving no half-written file.
  - `readFile`, `writeFile`, `pushTar`, `pullTar` and `pullTarStream` go past one call's limit; a pull reads a chunk only as its loop asks, so it can be piped into a push on the same connection. New error codes `exists`, `not_dir` and `is_dir`.
  - The new daemon code copies from E2B envd and Kubernetes sandboxd (Apache-2.0), recorded in UPSTREAM.json.
- An agent's computer is a setting kind: `none`, `sandbox`, `desktop`, `phone` or `browser` (`src/kinds.ts`). `SETTING_KINDS` gives each kind its machine kinds and its required and optional capability groups, and carries no tool names. `serves`, `servingRefusals` and `kindOf` are computed from a provider's capabilities, so a provider that drops a group stops serving the kind. `PROVIDERS` is what Settings offers. Each entry holds an id, a label, the serve-side key names (required or not), mounts, whether the vendor meters, and static capabilities, and its kinds are derived from those. Browserbase and Kernel are data until their provider files land. Each provider module exports `CAPABILITIES`, which must match its entry.

  - The protocol gains the machine kind `browser` (os linux, daemon none, Core group `cdp`), and the groups `cdp`, `stream` and `downloads`, which belong to the provider and never appear in a computerd hello. `ComputerConnection.cdp?: { url }` carries a browser's CDP target as a secret. `ComputerErrorCode` gains `unauthorized` and `unreachable`.
  - `testConnection()` on a provider makes one account-level read that never creates a computer. Docker, Daytona, E2B and Modal implement it. The first three count their own computers from one listing by their own label (one `docker ps`, one page), with no read-back of each. Modal reads its app without creating it. It answers `unauthorized` for a key the vendor refused.
  - Conformance runs by kind: `--kind <kind>` (`kind` in `runConformance`) runs `kindCases(kind)`. A provider passes the kind (`report.kindVerdict`, exit 0) only when it serves the kind, no case fails, each required group has a passing case, and no Core case of one skips. A Full case the provider refuses in words, such as the phone harness's pinch, may skip. `kindCases` and `kindVerdict` are exported for the serve side.
- `ModalProvider` makes computers that are Modal sandboxes on the VM runtime, through the host's own Modal client: the package names the slice of the JS SDK it calls (`ModalSdk`) and imports nothing from it. It has not run against real Modal yet; its tests use a fake of that slice.
  - The daemon is uploaded at create, so nothing is published: the sandbox's command is a POSIX sh entrypoint that waits, bounded, for `/opt/agentrun/computerd` and then `exec`s `computerd init --deadline <iso> --user <uid:gid>`; right after create the provider streams `dist/computerd-linux-amd64` (`daemonPath`) into `sh -c` through `exec`'s stdin in binary mode, which writes it to a part file, prints its sha256 and moves it into place only when it matches. A failed or mismatched upload terminates the sandbox and refuses. `modalCommands()` gives the commands as sent. With `daemonImage` (a registry image pinned by digest) the daemon instead comes in as a layer (`COPY --from`) and is the command itself. `connect()` runs `computerd stdio` through `exec`, so a computer with network none is reached and needs no network for its daemon.
  - Capabilities: kind `linux`, daemon `pid1` (init is the sandbox's main process, and Modal finishes the sandbox when it exits), groups `exec`, `files` and `processes`, `maxLifetimeMs` the instance's cap (24 hours, Modal's longest timeout, or less with the `maxLifetimeMs` option; `check()` refuses a longer lifetime with the shared `checkLifetime()`), lifetime `vendor` (Modal's timeout is the lifetime), network `none` or `open` with `networkReadBack: false`, isolation `vm`, os `linux`, user `uid` only. An allowlist, `pids`, a GPU and a change of network are refused in words. `release(ref, "stop")` is refused: Modal has no stop that keeps a sandbox; `delete` terminates and is idempotent, and since Modal ends a sandbox asynchronously it then polls, with pauses that double from `timeouts.releasePollMs` (500 ms) to 5 s, until `poll()` reads an exit code or Modal no longer knows the sandbox, within `timeouts.releaseMs` (120 s) and the caller's signal; past the bound it is a `timeout` that says what `poll()` last read. `list()` polls each sandbox Modal lists and leaves out one that has finished.
  - The drift check after `create()` and in `attach()`: the sandbox runs, its tags read back as given, hello's version, os, arch, kind and exact groups match, and `computerd probe`, run through the daemon, reports the computer's user, a kernel that is not gVisor's, and, for network none, no connection to its public addresses. A mismatch terminates the sandbox at create, and create waits, as release does, until `poll()` reads it finished, so a refused create (drift, an abort, a failed upload) leaves nothing listed; the terminate is sent whatever the caller's signal, and the wait, bounded by `timeouts.releaseMs`, ends on the caller's abort (an aborted create returns at once); past the bound or on an abort the refusal says the sandbox may still be ending and what `poll()` last read. `attach()` refuses and leaves it running. `network(ref)` reads network none back with the same probe.
  - The daemon's path is root's alone, every directory up to `/`: the upload refuses a path another user can change (not owned by uid 0, group or other writable, or a link) with nothing installed, makes `/opt/agentrun` root's 0755, and checks the installed path again; with `daemonImage`, a `RUN` step right after the `COPY` holds the image's path to the same rule (a path another user can change fails the build). So the bridge each connection runs as root through Modal's exec is root's own.
  - A Modal computer never runs as root: `check()` and `create()` refuse a `user` of `"0"`, `"0:<gid>"`, `"root"` or `"root:<gid>"` in words, before any Modal call, and `connect`, `attach` and `network` refuse a computer whose spec tag records root before they run anything in it. Every connection is Modal's exec of `/opt/agentrun/computerd stdio`, outside the daemon, with Modal's root capabilities; root without a capability still owns `/`, `/opt` and `/opt/agentrun`, so it could rename another program over that path. A user that is not root writes none of them (the daemon is `0:0 755` in a `0:0 755` directory). The conformance case `exec.root-on-request`, which needs `user:root`, skips on Modal as undeclared.
  - Every SDK call is bounded by a timeout and takes the caller's signal; a create that gives up has its sandbox terminated if one lands later, and a bridge whose exec lands after its connection gave up (past its bound, or on the caller's abort) has its stdin closed as it lands. One deadline, `helloMs`, covers a connection's exec of the bridge (at most `callMs` of it) and the hello after it.
  - `computerd init` takes `--user <uid:gid>`: it starts as root, gives `$HOME` and the socket's directory to that user (0700) and becomes it, with no supplementary groups.
  - `computerd init` drops its privileges itself, as any user, root included, with or without `--user`: on every thread it sets `no_new_privs`, empties the bounding set (each capability up to `/proc/sys/kernel/cap_last_cap`), the ambient set and the effective, permitted and inheritable sets, and reads it all back. Where a capability cannot be dropped (no CAP_SETPCAP with a bounding set), or in a binary that links cgo, init refuses to start, in words. That is policy, failing closed: a vendor that starts it as a non-root user with a bounding set it cannot empty (a Kubernetes `runAsUser` without dropping ALL capabilities) gets no computer rather than one with capabilities init cannot prove gone.
  - The conformance cases `exec.unprivileged` (Core), `exec.root-on-request` and `exec.user-uid` (Full) require `CapEff`, `CapPrm`, `CapInh`, `CapBnd` and `CapAmb` all zero and `NoNewPrivs` 1 in `/proc/self/status`, where they required only `CapEff` and `CapPrm` zero.
  - `waitUntil(read, done, { boundMs, signal, minMs, maxMs, what, describe })` is a bounded wait with doubling pauses that returns the first state `done` accepts, or a timeout or abort naming the last state read. Modal's one wait after a terminate, `untilFinished`, is built on it, and release, a refusing create and the Modal sweep all wait through that. `computerd probe [--connect <host:port>]...` prints the uid, gid, kernel release and the addresses it reached.
  - `ComputerCapabilities.networkReadBack` says whether `network()` reads the policy back from outside the computer (Docker: true). `ComputerErrorCode` gains `"modal"`.
  - `dist/conformance/modal.js` is a provider module for the conformance CLI, and `npm run test:modal` the live suite; both run only with `AGENTRUN_COMPUTER_LIVE_MODAL=1`, `COMPUTER_MODAL_TOKEN_ID` and `COMPUTER_MODAL_TOKEN_SECRET` set. The module passes that pair to `ModalClient` explicitly and never uses `MODAL_TOKEN_ID` or `MODAL_TOKEN_SECRET`, which may hold a production token; with only those set it refuses in words. The key goes only to `COMPUTER_MODAL_SERVER_URL`, else the SDK's default server: the module refuses while `MODAL_SERVER_URL` is set and pins the server while it constructs the client, so no `MODAL_PROFILE` or `.modal.toml` profile can redirect it. It reads `COMPUTER_MODAL_APP` and, optionally, `COMPUTER_MODAL_DAEMON_IMAGE`; without it the daemon is uploaded. The drifted provider uploads a daemon built with Go as `<version>-drift`. Every computer of a run lives at most 30 minutes.
  - `dist/conformance/modal-sweep.js --before <file>` records the app, the server and every running sandbox in the app carrying `agentrun.computer=1`; `--after <file>` refuses a file of another app or server, or one naming neither, before it lists anything, else terminates every such sandbox not in that file, reads each back through `untilFinished` until it has finished (60 s each), prints `{"swept":n,"gone":m,"stillThere":[ids]}`, and exits 0, 1 (one still runs) or 2 (usage, another app or server, token, refused list; nothing terminated). It reads only the `COMPUTER_MODAL_` token, app and server, the way the module does.
  - `ComputerCapabilities.release` declares how release may end a computer: Docker `["stop", "delete"]`, Modal `["delete"]`. Core `provider.stop` holds a provider to it: one that declares stop lists the computer stopped; one that does not refuses `release(ref, "stop")` as `unsupported`, in words, and the computer stays listed and running. The Full `provider.janitor` case also needs `release:stop`. A provider written against `ComputerProvider` adds `release` to its `capabilities()`.
- A computer mounts the run's folder at `/workspace` (`WORKSPACE`), the one workspace path in every computer. `ComputerSpec.mount` is `{ kind: "bind", path }` (a folder on the provider's host) or `{ kind: "archil", disk, region, folder, token }` (the run's folder inside an Archil disk in its region), and `capabilities().mount` lists the kinds a provider takes. Each provider file exports `MOUNT` beside `ENV`, the same kinds (Docker `["bind"]`, Modal and Daytona `[]`), and `ProviderModule` has it, so a caller tells which provider binds a local folder without building one. `checkMount(mount, declared)` is the one rule every provider's `check` runs, with no vendor call: a kind the provider does not declare is refused in words; a bind's path is absolute, canonical, an existing directory, with no NUL; an Archil mount's disk, region, folder and token are non-empty strings, the folder relative to the disk's root with no `..`. The Archil token is never in a refusal, a label, a read-back (`MountReadBack`, `readBackOf`: `{ kind: "archil", disk, region, folder }`) or an error.
  - `DockerProvider` declares `mount: ["bind"]` and binds the folder read-write with `--mount type=bind,src=<path>,dst=/workspace`; `HOME` and the default workdir stay `/work`. It never chowns or chmods the folder: `create` refuses, after reading the host and before `docker run`, a folder its computer's user (the spec's, or `docker.user`) cannot write and search, by the owner's, group's or others' bits as the kernel picks them. On a host that remaps user ids (`docker info` names `userns` or `rootless`: `host().remap`) the folder's owner is not the computer's user, so a bind is refused there in words before any `docker run`, and by `check` once the host is read; a computer with no mount is made as before. `check` refuses an Archil mount and a path `--mount` cannot hold (a comma, a double quote, a line break).
  - `attach` and `list` read the mount back from `docker inspect` (`mountOf`): `{ kind: "bind", path }`, or `{ other: "<words>" }` for a read-only bind, a volume or two mounts at `/workspace`, which `attach` refuses. The drift check holds a computer to the bind it was asked for (that path, read-write, `rprivate`) and to no other mount.
  - The conformance suite has a `mount` group, Full until the cutover decides: `mount.run-folder-bind` and `mount.run-folder-archil` ("mounts the run's folder": a file written outside is read at `/workspace`, one written inside is read outside, and a program run with `cwd: "/workspace"` sees it; the mount reads back as asked, with no token) and `mount.undeclared-kind` (each kind a provider does not declare is refused by `check` and `create` in words). A provider module exports `mountFor(kind)` to give each case a fresh run folder (`RunFolder`), and `runConformance` takes `mountFor`; `--provider docker` gives a bind of a new folder under the temp directory.
- An Orgo desktop provider (`orgo` in the registry, its Settings entry the desktop kind with `ORGO_API_KEY`, no mount, metered): `create({ apiKey, workspaceId })` makes Linux desktops on Orgo with computerd inside, on Orgo's own X display, so a desktop speaks the same protocol as every computer. It reads no environment: serve passes `apiKey` from its `ORGO_API_KEY` setting. The module exports `CAPABILITIES` and `MOUNT` (`[]`), and `testConnection()` is one read of the workspace's computers, which makes nothing.
  - It declares a Linux desktop (exec, files, processes, screen, input.pointer, input.keys), daemon child, network `"none"`, lifetime `janitor` (Orgo ends a computer only in whole hours, so `ends_in_hours` is the backstop and the janitor deletes it at its exact deadline), release `delete`, `extend`, `vendorAgentPorts` (Orgo's VNC 5999, websockify 6080 and desktop API 8080), and no mount yet.
  - Orgo has no egress control, so the tools' isolation is init's own network namespace (`computerd init --own-network`): network none is read back from inside, by a probe refused at the outside and, on both loopbacks, at the declared ports and every TCP port root listens on in Orgo's own network (read at create and named in its notes).
  - Before init starts, `create` makes every path unix socket of Orgo's desktop root's alone (owner root, mode 0600) but the display's own and computerd's, and names them in its notes: the tools reach the X server and nothing else. A socket it cannot lock, or a scan that fails, refuses the create and deletes the computer. Root's D-Bus buses were open to the tool user, and polkit over the system bus is a known privilege door.
  - Each deployment's computers live in one Orgo workspace. A computer's name, `computer-<12 hex>-r<the run's hash>-e<the epoch>-d<its deadline in base-36 seconds>`, lets `list`, the takeover and the janitor filter by run and epoch and read the deadline with nothing run inside; `extend` renames it (PATCH) with the deadline init took, a computer's extends run in turn, the name's deadline never moves back, and a ref made before still reaches it. Every label and the deadline are in the root-owned `/opt/agentrun/labels.json`, which `attach` reads as the truth.
  - `create` refuses, and deletes, a computer whose daemon's sha256 differs, whose X server has no path socket (`/tmp/.X11-unix/X99`: the tools' network namespace cuts the abstract one), whose daemon drifts (its network not its loopback alone included), or whose probe reaches an address. An `extend` to a deadline earlier than the current one is refused in words.
  - Everything inside a computer goes through an `OrgoChannel` (`run`, `start`, `stdio`, `upload`); the default is Orgo's bash and upload calls and its root terminal WebSocket, made raw, so SSH can replace it. The key is only in the Authorization header, and the VNC password only in the terminal socket's URL.
  - The conformance module (`--provider orgo`) runs behind `AGENTRUN_COMPUTER_LIVE_ORGO=1`, reads `COMPUTER_ORGO_API_KEY`, `COMPUTER_ORGO_WORKSPACE`, `COMPUTER_ORGO_API_URL` and `COMPUTER_ORGO_TEMPLATE` in `settingsFromEnv` (listed in its `ENV`), and caps every computer at 1 h.
- The phone kind: `src/phone-harness.ts`, provider `phone-harness` in the registry (`providerFor("phone-harness", options)`), rents a temporary cloud Android from the phone harness (phone-harness.com, 5 cents a minute) and drives it over adb. `create(options)` takes the API key as `apiKey` (serve passes `PHONE_HARNESS_API_KEY`), and `adb`, `fetch`, `ledger` and the clock as options. No daemon runs on the phone: `connect()` gives a client in this process that answers `screen`, `input.touch`, `input.keys`, `apps` and `apps.ui_tree` with adb commands, and refuses exec, files, processes, pinch, hold and key combos in words. The phone harness keeps no labels, so the provider keeps them in its `ledger` (`{ put, get, list, remove }`; in memory by default), and list, attach, release and the janitor see only the phones it names. Its `testConnection` is one `GET /sessions`, which rents nothing. A cloud iPhone is refused in words for now. With no fixture app, the conformance suite's touch, keys and apps cases drive the stock Settings app and read back through `apps.ui_tree`, and a new Core case, `apps.launch-terminate`, which needs the apps group alone, lists, launches and terminates it, and sees the launch in `apps.ui_tree` when the phone has one, else in a screenshot that changed. A run's computers ask for at most the provider's `maxLifetimeMs`. `ComputerErrorCode` gains `phone`. The conformance module reads `COMPUTER_PHONE_HARNESS_*` and `AGENTRUN_COMPUTER_LIVE_PHONE_HARNESS`.
- Network none, read from inside a computer, now holds when nothing answers within 10 s: `computerd probe` counts a target reached only when bytes come back from it, not when a TCP connection is accepted. A target is `host:port`, tried over TCP, or `host:port/udp`, sent a DNS query over UDP. Each TCP target gets a request by its port (443 a TLS ClientHello, with no server name for an IP address; 80 an HTTP `HEAD`; 53 a DNS query; any other port an HTTP `HEAD`); a UDP target gets one DNS query. Each has 10 s to connect and 10 s to answer, and at most 4 KiB is read. Any byte back is an answer: a ServerHello, a TLS alert, an HTTP status line, a DNS response with the query's id, a banner, or bytes of no expected protocol. UDP has no accept, so a UDP target with no reply is refused. The probe's JSON line keeps `reached` (now the targets that answered) and adds `accepted` (a connection accepted, then nothing back) and `results` (each target's protocol, outcome, bytes and detail).
  - A provider refuses network none only on an answer. A connection accepted with nothing back is recorded, never refused and never hidden: `create` now returns `CreatedComputer`, a `ComputerRef` with optional `notes`, and `attach`'s `AttachedComputer` gains `notes`, such as `network: none; accepted with no answer (no bytes came back): 1.1.1.1:443`. This is for vendors whose firewall accepts every outbound connection and then closes a denied one (E2B documents this). The conformance report gains `computerNotes` (`[{ computer, note }]`, absent when empty), every note a create returned, and the text report prints each as `note <computer>: <words>` above the verdict.
  - What the rule can and cannot detect: it detects a TCP or UDP path that carries bytes back within 10 s on a probed host:port. It cannot detect a one-way leak, a port that is not probed, or a client-first server that silently drops what it is sent (such a TCP target reads `accepted`; only an operator's own `probeTargets` can meet one).
  - The Modal provider is the one that probes today. Its default targets add DNS over UDP to `1.1.1.1:53/udp` and `[2606:4700:4700::1111]:53/udp`, its `probeTargets` option takes `host:port/udp`, and a probe report with no `accepted` list is refused in words. Its 30 s bound on the probe covers the probe's longest run (20 s). Docker and Daytona read their network back from the vendor, and a probe there is refused outright, so their results do not change.
- computerd's process surface takes what a background shell tool needs, with no script run in the computer.
  - `process.start` takes `log`: an absolute path for a new file the daemon makes and writes the first 16 MiB of the output to, stdout and stderr as they come. It makes the missing directories and the file itself and never follows a link (each directory entered with `O_NOFOLLOW|O_DIRECTORY`, the file made with `O_CREAT|O_EXCL|O_NOFOLLOW`, mode 0644): a link at the path or at a directory on its way is refused as `denied`, a file already there as `exists`, and nothing starts.
  - `process.read` reads the `log` stream from the daemon's own open file, never by path, and says when the log was `capped`; every read answers `size`, the stream's bytes so far. A write to the log that fails or writes short (a full disk) stops it for good, so the file stays a true prefix of the output, and the read's `logError` says why.
  - `process.start` takes `waitGroup` (the process runs until its program and every process left in its group, on a terminal its session, have ended, and reports the program's own exit; a signal reaches each of its process groups once) and `orphans: "keep"` (what it started outside that runs on, unsignalled, to the computer's end).
  - Conformance cases `process.log`, `process.log-links`, `process.wait-group` and `process.orphans-keep`.
- A process keeps running across calls and connections: `process.start` (argv, with an optional pty and stdin), `process.read` by byte offset with an optional bounded wait, `process.write`, `process.signal` (`TERM`, `INT`, `KILL`, `HUP` to the program's group), `process.resize`, `process.list` and `process.wait`. Hello's capabilities gain `processes`.
  - `computerd init` now serves the protocol on a unix socket (`/run/agentrun/computerd.sock`, socket 0600) with one process table for every connection, and `computerd stdio` is a bridge to it that fails in words when no daemon listens. `DockerProvider.connect()` is unchanged. Init needs the socket's directory there already, the daemon's user's alone (0700) and writable, and exits 1 in words otherwise; `DockerProvider.create()` mounts `/run/agentrun` as a tmpfs owned by the computer's uid:gid, mode 0700, `noexec,nosuid,nodev`.
  - Each stream keeps its last 8 MiB; a read below the ring's start reports the bytes `dropped`. All rings together hold at most 256 MiB (`PROCESS_RETAINED_BYTES`): past it, the rings of the processes that ended first are let go, never a running one's, and a read of one reports its bytes `dropped`, with the process still listed and its exit status kept. The table holds 256 processes; the one that ended first is dropped for a new start once its program runs (a refused start drops nothing), and a start is refused as `limit` when all 256 run.
  - `exec.run`'s `timeoutMs` counts from the call, the start included: a guard that never reports the program's start is killed with everything under it, and the run times out. Starts take turns: a `process.start` cancelled while it waits for its turn, or before its program runs, is `aborted`, with nothing it started left.
  - Writes to one process take turns; a write's 10 s bound and its cancel cover its wait for its turn.
  - Every method names a signal the same way, without `SIG` (`"TERM"`, `"KILL"`): `exec.run`, `process.wait` and `process.list`.
  - `process.list {from?}` answers `{processes, truncated, next}` in pages of at most 768 KiB, paged by start order (list again `from: next`), so a process dropped between pages skips no other; it lists at most 64 KiB of each argv (`argvClipped` when cut). `listProcesses` pages it whole.
  - Init also ends an orphan whose guard died (a program can kill its own guard), with everything under it, and on SIGTERM, or at its `--deadline`, ends every process and exits 0; a guard its shutdown reaps first is reported as ended by `KILL`. `readAll` pages a stream until its process ends.
- The provider registry: a new computer is one provider file and one registry line, resolved through it by the conformance CLI (and by serve, once wired). `src/providers.ts` maps each id to a lazy import of its provider file (`docker`, `modal`, `daytona`), so loading it loads no provider and no vendor SDK; `providerFromEnv(id, env?)`, `providerModule(id)`, `providerIds` and the `ProviderModule` and `ConformanceModule` types are exported from the package, and an unknown id is refused naming the ones the registry holds.
  - Each provider file exports `ENV`, the variables it reads, every one `COMPUTER_<ID>_*`, and `fromEnv(env, options?)`, the provider built from them alone. The env reading moved out of the conformance modules into the provider files with its rules unchanged: Modal takes its key from `COMPUTER_MODAL_TOKEN_ID` and `COMPUTER_MODAL_TOKEN_SECRET` only, pins the server it goes to, and refuses while `MODAL_SERVER_URL` is set; Daytona never reads `DAYTONA_API_KEY`; Docker reads `COMPUTER_DOCKER_DAEMON` (default this package's daemon for the architecture `docker version` reports, bounded at 30 s) and `COMPUTER_DOCKER_USER`. Modal's live switch, the 30-minute run cap, Daytona's run bounds, snapshot and notes, and the drifted providers stay in the conformance modules, which build through `fromEnv`; the three copies of the drifted daemon's Go build are one.
  - Behaviour change: the Docker conformance module now honours `COMPUTER_DOCKER_USER` and `COMPUTER_DOCKER_DAEMON` (before, it read neither).
  - The conformance CLI resolves `--provider <id>` through the registry for every id, Docker included, and its usage lists the registry's ids; a bare name in any case that the registry does not hold (`Docker`) is refused as an unknown provider, not read as a module path. A module path still works, and may be a provider file that exports `fromEnv()`, run with no drifted provider. `dockerConformance()` and its `close()` are gone: `dist/conformance/docker.js` exports `createProvider()` and `createDriftedProvider()` like the others, and the CLI's `dockerTimeoutMs` option is now `providerOptions`, given to the module's functions.
- `DockerProvider` finds, checks and sweeps computers. `attach({ id } | { labels })` finds exactly one computer, `list(labels)` reads back each one's labels, deadline, state and network, and `network(ref, policy?)` reads the network back (`none`, `open`, or anything else in words) and refuses any change, since a Docker computer's network is fixed at create.
  - `create()` ends with a drift check, `drift(spec, inspected, hello, expected)`: the network, dropped capabilities, `SecurityOpt` exactly `["no-new-privileges"]`, private IPC and cgroup namespaces, the `runc` runtime, Docker's masked and read-only paths, the pids, memory and CPU quota (with serve's defaults when the spec names none), the user it runs as, the image, workdir and `HOME`, exactly the `/work` and `/run/agentrun` tmpfs mounts with that user's uid and gid, mode 0700 and their sizes, the read-only daemon mount and no other mount (an image's `VOLUME` included), the entrypoint and `init --deadline <the label's deadline>`, the labels, the host's AppArmor profile or SELinux label, and hello's version, os, capabilities and architecture must match what was declared, or the computer is deleted and `create()` refuses with every mismatch in words.
  - The drift check is closed: every other `HostConfig` and `Config` field, one a later Docker adds included, must be Docker's empty default (`PidMode`, `UsernsMode`, `Devices`, `Sysctls`, `OomKillDisable`, `PortBindings`, `ExtraHosts` and the rest), but for a few with a stated default (no restart policy) and a few that widen nothing (the log driver, `ShmSize`, `Ulimits`, the image's own settings). `inspectDrift` is its inspect half. `create` now passes `--ipc private --cgroupns private`.
  - `host()` and `hostOf(info)` read the Docker host once per provider: its architecture, AppArmor and SELinux, and whether it enforces memory, swap, pids and CPU limits and has seccomp, not unconfined. `create()` and `attach()` refuse a host that lacks any of these in words before any other Docker call ("this Docker host does not enforce memory limits, so a computer's memory size is only configuration"), and `check()` does too once the host is read.
  - `attach()` runs the same drift check on what it finds, inspect and, when the computer runs, hello, against the spec read back from the computer and held to what `check()` allows, and refuses a computer that drifts with every mismatch, leaving it as it is.
  - `/work` is a tmpfs of half the computer's memory (`size=2048m` by default) and `/run/agentrun` one of 1 MiB, so filling `/work` fails with "No space left on device" instead of an OOM kill.
  - The provider's `user` option refuses root's group as well as root (`"1000:0"` throws), and the default user is this process's uid:gid only when neither is 0, else `1000:1000`. A spec may still ask for gid 0.
  - `create(spec, { signal })` honours the signal throughout: an abort during `docker info` makes nothing, and one while inspect or hello is pending deletes the computer and returns `aborted`, never ok.
  - `list()` inspects 50 computers at a time and reads only the fields it reports, so a large fleet is listed (and swept) whole.
  - `connect()` refuses a daemon of another version, or one missing a capability the provider declares.
  - `sweep(provider, { labels?, now? })` deletes the computers of a `lifetime: "janitor"` provider whose deadline has passed or is missing, stopped (as a computer is once its deadline ends it) or running, and keeps the rest; it refuses a provider whose vendor ends computers itself. A caller runs it on a schedule.
- `@parcha/agentrun-computer` is no longer held: the next release publishes it (`npm install @parcha/agentrun-computer@latest`), in the first layer, since it depends on no other package. Its archive holds `dist`, with the daemon for Linux amd64 and arm64 (`dist/computerd-linux-amd64` and `-arm64`, executable) and the Docker egress proxy's code (`dist/docker/egress-main.js`, `egress-proxy.js` and `egress-table.js`, which the proxy container runs from a bind of `dist/docker` and `package.json`), the README, LICENSE and NOTICE. A new subpath, `@parcha/agentrun-computer/mount`, exports the mount rule alone (`WORKSPACE`, `MOUNT_KINDS`, `checkMount`, `readBackOf`, and the types `MountSpec`, `MountReadBack` and `MountKind`): importing it loads `dist/mount.js` and node's built-ins, and no provider, vendor SDK or conformance code. A provider's mount kinds are read with `(await providerModule(id)).MOUNT`, which loads that one provider's file. The package check (`scripts/verify-packages.mjs`) requires both daemon binaries in the archive, each an ELF for its architecture stamped with the package's version, and the egress proxy's three files, imports the root and `./mount` in the clean consumer, checks in a fresh process that `./mount` loads nothing else, runs the installed egress proxy laid out as its container binds it, with nothing beside it, and compiles a TypeScript consumer of both entry points; the release's receipt carries a `computer` smoke.
