A file tool's absolute path under the run's own work directory on this machine names the same file in the box. `AGENTRUN_DOCKER_IMAGE` names the image (default `debian:bookworm-slim`). The console leaves `bash` unticked on a new agent and offers it only on an agent whose place is a sandbox. In pi-transport, `dockerBoxes({ user })` runs a box's programs as that uid:gid, and `endLeftCommands(box)`, from `@parcha/pi-transport/hands`, kills the command groups a gone host left in a box, leaving a tombstone for one not written down yet.
- An agent whose place is `{"mode": "sandbox", "box": "daytona"}` runs its `read`, `write`, `edit` and `bash` in a Daytona sandbox, on pi, claude-agent and codex alike, as a Docker place does. The server holds Daytona's key (`DAYTONA_API_KEY`, with `AGENTRUN_BOXES=daytona`) and gives it to nothing else: it makes the run's sandbox on the run's first such call, with its network blocked (`networkBlockAll`; a sandbox Daytona does not report blocked is deleted and the call fails), the fixed environment and the run's labels, and the run's process reaches that sandbox only through the server's box relay on loopback, which takes a run's call only with that run's own door token, adds the key to the calls a run's tools make of their own sandbox (a read of it, and its toolbox's programs and files), refuses every other call, and never follows a redirect (nor does pi-transport's Daytona client): a redirect would carry the key. Each run's door now takes a token of its own, `runs/<id>/door-token`, made at its first launch, and a run's end-of-run notes that hold it are refused like any other secret. A sandbox cannot bind the run's `work/`, so the server copies `work/` into it at `/run/agentrun/run/work` when it makes it, copies what changed back at each turn's end and once more before the run's end is recorded (with pi-transport's safe extractor: nothing outside `work/`, no link, a file the server changed meanwhile kept), and destroys it only once that last copy is whole: a copy that fails is tried again before the end is recorded, and one that stops for good (an archive the server cannot read, a limit it cannot pass) keeps the sandbox, said in the log, its auto-delete turned off and set to stop after 15 idle minutes (its disk kept, its compute stopped), woken and copied back by the next start. A run's sandbox that Daytona stopped, paused or archived (or is stopping, pausing or archiving) is started again, an archived one restored, when the server needs it, never replaced. A read of the run's files waits for a copy its turn's end began, and a start pulls a run it finds waiting. What the server files in `work/` meanwhile (monid's long answers) is copied into the sandbox too, and an edit the sandbox makes to such a file comes back. A start pulls, then destroys, the sandbox of a run that ended while its sandbox was left. In pi-transport, a Daytona box takes `network: "none"` (`networkBlockAll`, and `facts().network` is `none` only when Daytona reports it), a workspace the sandbox's user may not create is made with `sudo -n`, and a paused sandbox counts as not running (a host starts it again).
- **Workflows on AgentRun Server read documents, call one completion, judge images, and run code nodes when allowed**
  - `pdf_pages`, a workflow call tool: `{ path, maxPages?, dpi? }` draws a document's first pages as PNGs under the run's `work/pages/<stem>/<dpi>/` (long side at most 1568 px, Jev's largest) with its text. A PNG or JPEG passes through, scaled down if larger. `path` is confined to `--documents DIR` (or `AGENTRUN_SERVE_DOCUMENTS`) and the run's `work/`. Needs poppler's `pdftoppm` and `pdftotext`.
  - `llm`, a workflow call tool: one completion on the run's model of the call's tier, no tools and no loop: `{ text, model, usage, cost_usd }`, the cost null when the model has no price.
  - Both are call-only: an agent or a model node naming them is refused (`422`), and `GET /capabilities` marks them `call_only`.
  - Decision nodes' `images` are loaded from the run's `work/`. Nodes naming `provider: "openrouter"` run on OpenRouter's Decisions models, on the OpenRouter key Jev's OpenRouter route reads (`OPENROUTER_API_KEY`, else the gateway's `AGENT_MODEL_API_KEY` on openrouter.ai), handed on the decision descriptor; capabilities list `openrouter` among the providers (under `faux`, startServe's `fauxOpenrouter` fakes it).
  - A call tool that fails on its own fails that item in a collecting `map` instead of the run.
  - `code` nodes run only on a server started with `--allow-code-nodes` (or `AGENTRUN_SERVE_CODE_NODES=1`): they run in the run's process through `new Function`, which is no sandbox. Off (the default), such a workflow saves with a `not_set_up_here` warning and its runs are `409 agent_not_runnable` with `details.code: true`. Calls via a shell or the executor stay refused.
  - Pi's decision rows keep a receipt's images (by digest), the state the judge saw and its cost basis, and a replay returns them.
- **A workflow run on AgentRun Server is drawn live, pauses and resumes, and a workflow is checked before it is saved**
  - `GET /v2/runs/{id}/graph`: the run's workflow and graph, each node's state (map items labelled from the run's input, with the branch each route took for them; a route's branch), the decisions with their receipts (state seen, images, evidence, cost basis, error), the calls with their input and output, and totals. While the run goes it reads a live view the run's process writes (`live.json`), so a poll never waits on the run's store or answers `home_busy`.
  - `POST /v2/runs/{id}/pause` and `/resume`: what is in flight finishes, and nothing new starts until the resume; a paused run stays paused across a restart. Run status gains `paused` (marked `pause/1`), `GET /runs?status=paused` lists them, and `GET /stats` counts a paused run as active.
  - `POST /v2/workflows/validate { workflow, tools? }` answers `{ ok, issues: [{ path, code, message }], warnings }` and writes nothing. A refused save's `422` lists every issue as a field (`definition.workflow/<path>`) with its `code`.
  - A workflow run's title is its agent's name and a few words of its input, never its JSON. A run's `llm` calls count in its cost.
  - pi-durable-agent: `workflowIssues(workflow, tools?)` lists every issue a save would refuse; `checkWorkflowBody`'s refusal carries them, each with its message.
  - dsl: the recovery driver's `pinJson()` gives its frame as it stands, for a host's live view.
- `npx @parcha/agentrun` stops its server when the npm that runs it ends: a SIGTERM or SIGKILL to npm alone (a process manager's) used to leave the command and the server running with nobody to stop them. Only a command that npm exec started watches its npm; started any other way (an installed `agentrun`, in the background of a shell that then exits, under nohup), it keeps running as before. A SIGINT to npm alone still reaches nothing, since npm leaves SIGINT to the terminal's process group: stop it with Ctrl-C in its terminal, a signal to its process group, or a SIGTERM to npm. A SIGHUP to the command alone now stops the server as a SIGTERM does, and the server drains each run once.
- The console names an agent made since the page loaded, here or elsewhere, wherever its runs show: the runs list and a run's page read the agents' names again when they open, so a new agent no longer shows as its id until a reload. An archived agent's runs are named too; only a run whose agent is gone shows the agent's id, and a name being read shows as nothing yet, never as the id. A read that partly fails keeps the names it got.
- A browser step on a run's Timeline is labelled with what its tool does, where it read the tool's wire name ("run", "snapshot"): "Open or act on a page in the browser", "Look at the page structure", "Read the browser's page", and so on, the names the timeline library gives pi-browser's tools and a run's tool counts already show. A browser tool the library names nothing keeps its own name, and every other tool's row is unchanged.
- The console's `npm run dev` proxies `/v2` to an AgentRun Server on this machine at serve's default port when `AGENTRUN_URL` is not set, as it did before; `AGENTRUN_URL` still names any other server, and `AGENTRUN_TOKEN` still goes as a bearer header from the dev server only.
- The console's agent editor ties each label to its input: Name, Description, Instructions and Model are read by name by a screen reader, and a click on the label puts the cursor in the field.
- The console's agent editor fits a 375 px phone: the agent's name keeps the row, with its kind, revision count and Archive in a row under it, and the step rail shows step numbers (each step's name heads the step, and stays for screen readers), and the tool provider table (the Tools step, and Settings → Tools) scrolls in its own box instead of widening the page. An agent with no web tool, such as one that only reads and writes files, is no longer told on every step that it can't reach the web: the Tools step says so, quietly, in the same words.
- The console's effort picker says what it sets: how hard the model thinks. The agent page's run bar and the Playground say so beside the choice, and "default" (or empty) still leaves it to the agent.
- The console no longer hides two things the server says. A run that ended escalated (`failed`, its error's code `escalated`) reads Escalated in the runs list, Mission Control and the run's header, never Failed, and its error card says the run escalated. An agent save's `warnings` (what this host lacks to run the revision, such as a provider's key) now show after the save: every one is listed above the form, and each also appears beside the field it names. A create's warnings show on the agent's page that opens after it.
- The console tells a workflow run that escalated from one that was accepted. A run that completed with `review` (it waits for a person) reads "Needs review" in its badge, never a green "Completed". The run header and the Runs list say its reason, and its Report says the reason and the node it stopped at in place of "This run gave no answer". Mission Control's recent runs and an agent's runs follow the badge. Where `/v2/stats` counts such runs apart (`needs_review`), Mission Control says them under Failed and the analytics' outcomes read "completed · failed · need review", with a run waiting for review counted as no success. An older server's "failed or escalated" reads as before. A run graph's judge says its verdict with that verdict's own probability, as the timeline does ("no 0.99", not "no 0.01").
- The console's page declares its own icon, an orange square with a run mark, so the browser never asks for /favicon.ico and no page load logs a 404 for it.
- The console's agent editor shows the fetch provider row for an agent that grants `fetch_page`, web_fetch's older name, which the server runs as web_fetch. Before, such an agent had no row, so its fetch provider could be neither seen nor changed in the editor.
- Settings → Tool keys: a key that no harness reads (TypeSafe's, which only the decisions provider uses) says what it unlocks and nothing about harnesses, where it read "Unlocks decisions.jev · Harnesses" with nothing after the label. Mission Control's model step, where the server keeps no keys, has its spaces back: "in its environment, AGENT_MODEL=provider/model-id", where it read "environment,AGENT_MODEL=".
- The console shows a model without the gateway's internal routing prefix: on a server whose models come through a gateway (`capabilities.models.source` is `gateway`), the prefix every tier's model carries is read from `models.default` and taken off on the agent page and in the editor, with the full id in a tooltip. The editor's Model field no longer offers a harness a default it may not run: the server's default is offered for pi, and claude-agent and codex say "No default for this harness: choose a model", as the agent page says for one that names none. Until capabilities say which model each harness takes, a codex agent behind a gateway names its model by hand.
- Mission Control's no-model step points at the key card above it. Where the server keeps keys sealed, the first screen shows a card that saves a model key with no restart, yet the start-here panel under it still said to start the server with `AGENT_MODEL` and a provider's key in its environment, then reload. Now it says "Add a model key in the card above, and the templates here run in one click." Where the server keeps no keys, has no seal key to keep them with, names a default model in `AGENT_MODEL` (whose provider's key the card may not offer), runs on a gateway that names no model (`AGENT_MODEL_BASE_URL` with no `AGENT_MODEL`, which a provider's key doesn't fix), or its `AGENT_MODEL` is the problem (which no key fixes), the panel says what to set where the server starts, as before.
- The console's agent editor no longer offers or ticks a workflow's call-only tools (`pdf_pages`, `llm`: `call_only` in `capabilities.tools`) for an agentic agent, so a new agent created with its default tools saves instead of being refused with a 422. The console keeps each tool's `call_only` and `nodes` from capabilities; no harness runs a call-only tool, so an agentic agent's Tools step leaves them out, new and existing alike, and an existing agentic agent that names one shows it as workflow-only, ticked, so it can be unticked rather than dropped silently. A workflow agent still picks them: its call nodes need them listed in its revision's tools.
- When no default model is set, the console's no-model bar leads with saving a key in Settings where the server keeps keys (its key routes, with a seal key): "Add a key in Settings → Model keys (one OpenRouter key gives every harness its model), and it takes effect at once. Or set OPENROUTER_API_KEY, ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or OPENAI_API_KEY where AgentRun starts, and restart." Where the server keeps no keys, or has no seal key to keep one (`tool_keys.at_rest: "unavailable"`), it reads "Set OPENROUTER_API_KEY (one key gives every harness its model), or ANTHROPIC_API_KEY or OPENAI_API_KEY, and restart." When the server's `AGENT_MODEL` is set without its provider's key, an OpenRouter key does not run it, so the bar names that model instead: "This server's default model is <model>: set its provider's key where AgentRun starts, and restart.", with no Settings link, as Settings holds only some providers' keys. On a server that sends its models to a gateway (`models.source: "gateway"`) with no model named, it says what the server's own message does: "This server sends its models to a gateway: name the gateway's model in AGENT_MODEL where AgentRun starts, and restart, or add a model key in Settings → Model keys." (the Settings part only where the server can keep a key), with no OpenRouter promise. The bar for an `AGENT_MODEL` the server does not run is unchanged.
- The console's agent editor keeps a tool provider change made while a save is under way, a reset included. Before, picking a provider, saving, and resetting it before the save came back lost the reset: the form showed the saved provider again, and another save couldn't undo it.
- Console: an agent made here and run straight away in the Playground shows its name in the run panel's header on its first run, not its id. A run's short label (the run page's header, the Playground's run panel, a learned note's run) is now the last 8 characters of its id, so two runs started in the same minute no longer share a label. The file viewer also closes on a click on the dimmed backdrop around the file, as well as on its X and Escape; a click inside it, or a selection dragged out of it, keeps it open.
- The console's agent editor no longer tells an agent with `web_fetch` (or `web_search`) that it can't reach the web: any of `fetch_page`, `web_fetch` and `web_search` reaches it, one named list (`WEB_TOOLS`), since the server's capabilities mark no tool as web-reaching. An agent with none of them is told which of them its harness runs on this server, to tick, or that its harness runs none. The tools step shows what each tool does, from each tool's `summary` in capabilities.
- The console keeps everything the server says about a refused request: `ClientError` carries the error's `details`, and `toApiError()` passes them on. A run start refused with `409 agent_not_runnable` for a sandbox agent on a box the server does not have now shows in the playground as "The run did not start: <reason> (box: <box>)" above the whole answer, `details.box` included. The Files tab's comment now says that files list in the server's path order.
- The console's run timeline marks a failed tool call failed: it read a `tool_status` of `"error"`, which the open platform never writes, so a call that failed (or was interrupted) showed like one that succeeded. A workflow run's `decision` rows now read Decision with their own icon, and open to their cited sources and refused questions.
- Settings → Tools never shows a stale result beside a provider changed elsewhere: it reads the capabilities again as it opens, and a row whose provider the capabilities were not read for says "reading…" until they are, then offers Retry if a read failed or the provider moved again. A save is never undone by an older read.
- Settings → Tools says when reading the tool settings again fails, beside the table it keeps (the last settings it read), with Try again. Before, a Retry whose settings read failed showed nothing, and the row kept saying the provider had changed.
- A decision's deadline now counts the time its images take to load. `judgeFromProvider` and `judgeFromProviders` load a node's images within the provider's `deadlineMs` (or the node's shorter `timeoutMs`), and the provider gets what is left of it. Before, the clock started only once the images had loaded, so a `loadImage` that never returned held the decision forever. A load the deadline cuts fails `timeout`, and a load the run's cancel cuts fails `aborted`. So does a load that finishes past the deadline before its timer fires, for example while a loader's synchronous work holds the thread: the provider is not called with an expired budget. Nothing was sent in either case, so neither is `outcome_unknown`, and in a collecting map a `timeout` fails only its item. A `loadImage` that ignores its signal is cut too.
- In a map with `onItemFailure: "collect"`, a judge that fails inside a `parallel` fails its item again, not the run. The parallel cancels the sibling judge still in flight, and Jev and OpenRouter report that cancel as `DecisionProviderError("aborted")`, not as an `AbortError`. The parallel reported the sibling's `aborted` over the item's own failure, so the map stopped. It now reports the first failure, and the map writes that item's `failedAs` row and finishes the others. A run that is cancelled still stops the map.
- **`submitParameters` leaves `criteria` out of the submit tool's schema**
  - `criteria` is System One's question keyword: DSL vocabulary, not JSON Schema. A node's output schema could carry it into the submit tool the model sees, on every harness, and a harness that refuses unknown keywords refused the tool (the issue-triage example's `Judgment` schema as an agent output, for one). The envelope now drops it from every schema object it walks.
  - A property, `$defs` or `definitions` entry NAMED `criteria` keeps its name and its schema. Annotations (`title`, `description`, `examples`) and any other keyword stay as written.
  - Validation does not change: a runner and the interpreter check a submission against the node's schema as written, and `criteria` has no validation meaning, so no verdict differs.
- On a server started with `npx @parcha/agentrun`, a harness that is not set up (claude-agent without the Claude Agent SDK, codex without Codex) now says the one command that starts the same AgentRun again with it, on the same data: `npx -p @parcha/agentrun@<version> -p @parcha/agentrun-serve@<version> -p @anthropic-ai/claude-agent-sdk@0.3.286 agentrun --data ~/.agentrun` (with the store's absolute path). It keeps any other harness package already there, so setting up one never takes the other away. A server started with `npx @parcha/agentrun-serve` says the same with `agentrun-serve`, and adds `--data` only when its data is not in its default place. `npx @parcha/agentrun` keeps its data in `~/.agentrun` and hands it to the server, `import` and `reset-password` as `--data`, and its hint names that store as an absolute path, so the pasted line opens the same store from any directory; `agentrun-serve`'s own default, `~/.local/share/agentrun/agents`, does not change. When the server can't be downloaded, the command says so ("Couldn't download the AgentRun server (@parcha/agentrun-serve: not on npm). Your network or the release is the problem; nothing was started.") and exits 1, instead of saying the server did not start on a port.
- A decision row (`agentrun.decisions`) keeps what its receipt says beside the answers: `status`, `provider`, `evidence`, `refused` and `confidence_source`, each when present. A run resumed from its rows gets them back, so a replayed decision gives the same provider and sources its first run had.
- A record's scalar repair reads a field's `$ref` through the root schema's `$defs` as well as its `definitions`, at the top and in nested objects, so a string the referenced schema lists (a `const: "null"`) is kept as the value, never turned into JSON null. `parseStringifiedContainers`'s fourth argument is now the root schema (it was the root's `definitions`).
- A node's review that rejects a candidate without a message now rejects it on Pi. `createPiRunner`'s `submit` read the review's `message` as the rejection, so a review answering `{ accepted: false }` with none let the candidate through as the node's result and told the model it was accepted; a null, empty or blank message rejected with no words. Each now rejects, and the model reads that the review rejected it without saying why, as on the kernel's runner.
- A frozen run now records how many question answers came back with no price, so its recorded spend is read as incomplete rather than as a bare total that silently omits them. Before, an answer whose cost was null (or any non-finite or negative number) added nothing to `questionSpendUsd` and left no other trace, so the run's question spend read as a complete number that dropped the unpriced answer. An unknown cost is never zero.
  - The driver keeps `unpricedQuestionCount` beside `questionSpendUsd`, committed in the same save, and a resume that skips a finished unpriced question still reports it. A reader of the run's spend that finds the count non-zero has the known sum and the fact that it is incomplete.
  - A refused `recordQuestionSpend` (an answer for a node that is not a route, or a route already decided) now leaves the snapshot untouched, so a later save never carries a half-recorded answer forward.
  - The frozen checkpoint gains an optional `unpricedQuestionCount` (a non-negative integer). The checkpoint schema is unchanged, and a checkpoint written before this change has no count and opens as before.
- The repository's `npm run build` builds the console (`apps/console`) before the packages, so a fresh checkout's `npm ci && npm run build` gives a server that serves its console, not the "built without its console" page, and so does a release's build. It installs the console's dependencies when they are missing or its lockfile changed, and skips the build when `apps/console/dist` was built from the same sources (a hash of them, written beside the build, never file times). The console's own `npm run build` records that hash, so building it first, as CI and the image do, is not built twice.
- A script in `scripts/` run on a Node without `import.meta.main` (20, 23, 24.0 to 24.1) now exits 1 with one line naming the script, the Node it ran on and the Node the repository needs. Before, it exited 0 having done nothing, so `node scripts/changes.mjs --check` on Node 20 passed without checking anything. The scripts decide they are the command with `isMain(import.meta)` from `scripts/is-main.mjs`, which is `import.meta.main` where Node sets it; where Node does not, it compares the realpaths of `process.argv[1]` and the script, refuses the command, and leaves an imported module alone. `scripts/entry-points.test.mjs` fails on any other file that reads `import.meta.main` itself. The examples (copied into consumer and scratch projects), the package test fixtures (kept to their packages) and `apps/viewer/build.mjs` (deployed on its own) keep `import.meta.main`.
- A run runs where its agent's definition puts it: a run request names no place, and `POST /agents/{id}/runs` with `place` is `422 input_invalid` at `place`, as with any field the body does not keep. The served OpenAPI document has no `RunCreate.place`, and the console's run bar and playground offer no "Run on the server instead".
- `GET /v2/setup/import` answers `200 { "offer": false }` where it answered `404`: to the signed-in owner when no import was asked for at setup, and to the setup page of a server that cannot import (a public address, or open setup off loopback). The console reads that answer as no offer, so its first page no longer logs a failed request in the browser; it still reads a `404` from an older server the same way. An offer, its count and an import's state answer as before, and a browser that is not signed in still gets `404` once the owner is set.
- The source archive keeps source named `review`: a module `review.ts`, its `review.test.mjs`, and a skill directory named `review` were left out, so a package importing such a module did not build from the archive. Only review files (a file named `*.review.*`, such as the author's `<n>.review.json`) and the `.review` and `.reviews` directories are left out; a directory named like a review file is not.
- A fetch that a tool answered as refused or failed reads as that on the timeline, never as "ok". `web_fetch` and `fetch_page` answer a refusal (`{ok: false, refused}`, the public-host guard's) or a failure (`{ok: false, error}`) as their result rather than as an error, and the timeline read any returned call as "Fetched …: ok". Now such a call reads "Tried to fetch …", its result row says "refused: …" or "failed: …" with `tool_status` `failed`, and the text render lists it as failed, outside a fold of fetched pages. `fetch_page` gets the same label as `web_fetch` in place of "Using tool: fetch_page". A host registers how its own tools' results tell their outcome with `registerOutcome`, beside `registerLabel`. The console labels a `text_block` row "Text", with its own icon, in place of its kind's name.
- A run's tool counts read a call's outcome as its timeline does, and analytics name each tool in plain words. `runMetricsOf` counted a tool error only for an error result, so a refused or failed fetch (a result with `ok: false`) counted as a success while its row read failed. One rule, `callFailed(tool, result)` in the timeline's labels, now decides both. The label registry also keeps a tool's display name (`registerLabel(tool, label, fold?, { name })`, read with `nameOf`), and serve's `GET /v2/stats` sends it as `label` beside each `tools` row's `tool` (`stats/3`). The console shows the label, with the name the tool was called by as its title, so `monid_discover`, the browser's `run`, `browser_release` and `submit` read "Search monid's catalog", "Open or act on a page in the browser", "Release the browser session" and "Submit the answer". monid's tools also get timeline labels of their own.
- The test that damages a run's box ids directory before the supervisor's final pass now damages it when the run's process has exited, through a local place of the test's own, instead of from a file watcher. The watcher wrote the damaged file once `box-2.json` read `ended`, but nothing ordered its callback before the supervisor's reads: a supervisor pass could mark that receipt `written` first, so the watcher never wrote the file, or the file could land after the final pass. Either way the run succeeded and the test failed with "Missing expected rejection". The supervisor waits for the place's exit before it reads the directory again, so the damage now always lands after the process's last write and before the final pass. The reference demo's test `run()` takes demo options of a test's own as a fifth argument. Test-only: the harness is unchanged.
- CI builds once per Node version and shards the tests by time. A `build (<node>)` job runs `npm run build` and packs its output (every workspace's dist and the console's, about 10 MB) for the jobs that test it; each of those still runs `npm ci`, then unpacks the output and refuses one built from another tree or on another Node (`scripts/ci-build-output.mjs`). The test shards and the pi legs' parts get their files from `scripts/ci-plan.mjs`: each suite's test files, weighted by `scripts/ci-timings.json` (seconds from CI's own logs, refreshed with `node scripts/ci-timings.mjs <job logs>`), assigned longest first to the shards of their pool (`scripts/ci-shards.json`). Every shard job plans every shard and fails unless they cover each test file once, so a new package or test file runs without an edit. `checks` is split into `checks`, `packages` (verify-packages) and `source` (the downloaded source's consumer), which ran one after the other. The pi legs run as a consumer job and seven parts per pi version (`pi-leg.mjs --shard`), which build what they test themselves and do not wait for the build job. The required checks `verify (22.19.0)`, `verify (22.23.2)` and `lean` keep their names and what they gate.
- CI's advisory real-Docker job now also builds serve's default box image (python 3.12, git and curl, from its recipe) on the runner's Docker, checks its label, and runs `python3`, `git` and `curl` in a box made from it, which has no network. The step's per-file bound goes from 300 s to 900 s, inside the job's 30 minutes, for the build. It stays advisory: a failure shows and blocks nothing.
- **A registry connection that fails during `npm ci` no longer fails a CI job at once**
  - A registry request that fails before its response arrives (a connection reset, refused or timed out, or a 5xx) is retried 5 times, 20 seconds and then 2 minutes apart, where npm's default gave up after 2 retries in about 70 seconds. This holds for every `npm` and `npx` in the package checks, including the ones a script runs inside a job, except the stranger and source-consumer checks, which start npm with a clean environment on purpose.
  - npm never retries a download that drops midway. `node scripts/npm-ci-retry.mjs <flags>` runs `npm ci <flags>` and retries once on a network error only: when npm's error code is ECONNRESET, ETIMEDOUT, ECONNREFUSED, EAI_AGAIN, ENOTFOUND, EPIPE or ERR_SOCKET_TIMEOUT, or it reports a network error, it prints a `::warning::` naming the code, waits 15 seconds (`NPM_CI_RETRY_WAIT_MS`) and runs once more. Any other failure, a lockfile out of step with `package.json` or an engine npm refuses, and a second failure exit at once with npm's own code. Every `npm ci` step in the package checks runs through it, and so does the console's `npm ci` in `scripts/build-console.mjs`.
  - What it buys: a short registry outage costs a job up to about 8 minutes, or one extra install, instead of failing it and sending a merge-queue entry back to the end of the queue. The risk: a real registry outage fails a job a few minutes later than it did.
- The merge queue runs the test shards on Node 22.23.2 only; every pull request and every push to `main` still runs them on 22.19.0, 22.23.2 and 24.21.0. A queue run has 10 test jobs in place of 30, and a flaky test on the other two Node versions no longer ejects a change that already passed all three on its pull request. The TypeScript 5.4.5 floor check keeps running in the queue, on 22.23.2. The required checks `verify (22.19.0)` and `verify (22.23.2)` keep their names and run on every event. The risk: a change that breaks only on Node 22.19 or 24 after its pull request ran (a newer base) is caught on `main`'s push run, not in the queue.
- **A CI test that hangs fails by name, and a suite that never exits is killed, both before the test job's 30-minute limit**
  - `scripts/ci-shard.mjs` adds `--test-timeout` (10 minutes) to each test script of the shape `node --test [--test-concurrency=<n>] <files>`, and to the examples shards. On Node 24 a test still running then fails, named in the log; Node 22 applies the flag to each test file instead, and names the file. A test's own `timeout` holds on Node 24.
  - The scripts that run without it are pinned in `ci-shard.mjs` by their text: pi-durable-disk's (its own `--test-timeout`), pi-transport's vitest, pi-browser's and pi-vision's own runners, and `test:viewer`. A test script of any other shape fails its shard, naming the script, before anything runs.
  - A shard's suites get 24 minutes in all. The suite still running then is killed with every process it started, the suites after it are not run, and an `::error::` line names each. The job ends failed with its log, where a stalled shard used to be cancelled at 30 minutes, its log sometimes lost.
  - `CI_SHARD_TEST_TIMEOUT_MS` and `CI_SHARD_TIMEOUT_MS` set the two limits, in milliseconds.
- The Codex pin check (`verifyCodexBinary`) imports `sha256OfFile` and `checkedOnce` from `@parcha/agentrun-kernel/host`, the code the Claude adapter's check already uses, in place of a line-for-line copy inside the codex package. The copy and the test that held the two copies equal are gone. The check behaves as before. The image check's test now lays the kernel next to the codex adapter in its scratch tree, as npm does, since the adapter depends on it; without it, the test's Codex cases fail on the missing package.
- **A demo test file that leaves a child, a server or a held pipe open fails by name in seconds, instead of hanging its CI shard**
  - `node --test` waits for each file's process to exit before it reports the next file. One reference demo file whose tests all passed but whose process stayed alive held examples-2 until the 30-minute limit, with no name in the log.
  - The shared demo-test helpers now check each file that is still alive 10 seconds after its hooks start, after the file's own `after()` hooks have run. What is still open is named, closed, and the file fails: a child (by pid and argv) with the processes it started, a server (by address), or a pipe that a child's own child still holds.
- dsl's test of a poll wait with a fraction of a millisecond now fakes the clock and the timers the recovery driver waits on: after 200 ms of a 200.5 ms wait the next check has not started, and after 201 ms it has. Before, it measured the gap between two checks on `performance.now()` and wanted at least 199 ms, but Node can fire a timer a millisecond or two early on a loaded machine, so the test failed although the wait was right. pi-durable-disk's scripted `/proc` and `ps` tables now use pids from 9,004,200 up, past any pid Linux or macOS hands out. Before, they used pids such as 4300 and 4301, and when the test process itself had one of them, the reaper rightly refused to signal what it took for its own process or group, and the tests that expected those kills failed.
- agentrun-dsl drops eight tests that repeat a shared Lean conformance case, now that the `lean` check is required to merge: each case holds the Lean model and the TypeScript interpreter to the same verdict, state, error and events. From `lean-findings.test.mjs`: F1a (`requires-after-code`), F1b (`requires-empty-submission`), F2a (`parallel-code-collision`), F2b (`parallel-label-collision`), F3 (`code-writes-dollar-key`, `code-writes-host`), F7a (`parallel-host-scalar-then-array`, `parallel-host-array-then-scalar`) and F9 (`predicate-array-index`); F5 keeps only its typed-output half, since `child-undefined-output` holds the permissive one. From `predicate-not-field-type.test.mjs`: the single "abc" escalation (`escalate-not-field-type-fires`). Tests that check an error's wording, its position, or behaviour no case covers stay.
- **The ten-kill test covers every class of crossing with planted kills, not the seeds' draws**
  - A seeded kill lands at the nth crossing a process takes, and the order of those crossings shifts with timing. The seeds therefore did not always reach every class: a CI run missed `judge`.
  - `killAnywhere` now takes `planted` kills, each planned by point name on either side. A new test plants one kill at a point of each class, in the run's order, and asserts that each one lands there and that every class is covered.
  - The seeded runs keep every other check, and the draws are extras.
- The `lean` job takes about 2 minutes, down from about 16. It no longer runs the DSL, pi and example test suites a second time to collect the workflows for the validator sweep: on Node 22.23.2 the `core` and `examples-<n>` test shards record them while their tests run and keep them as an artifact, and `lean` waits for the test shards, reads their captures (`node spec/lean/sweep/collect.mjs --captures <dir>`), builds only the DSL and runs the proofs, the conformance cases and the sweep. That saves about 13 runner-minutes on every pull request, merge-queue and `main` run. The capture hook now runs in the test's own thread, which keeps the capturing shards at their usual speed (the off-thread hook made a DSL test file about a third slower), and each capture is written to a temporary file and renamed into place, so two test files recording the same workflow at once no longer leave a torn file that failed the sweep. The risks: `lean` now reports after the slowest test shard instead of on its own, and it fails when a capturing shard failed or its captures are missing, since the corpus would be incomplete. `lean` is not a required check, and `verify` does not need it.
- The `--open` demo test waits for the stub opener's whole line, not just its file: the stub's `>>` creates the file before its `printf` writes, so a read in between got an empty string and failed the test on a loaded CI runner. Its wait is also 15 s instead of 5 s.
- Two pi-durable-disk local-run tests that need a process the reap cannot kill (one refused open, one boxed open after an unboxed one) now use kthreadd on Linux. They borrowed the lowest-pid root process, which can end while the test runs. When it did, the reap found nothing left to wait for, the open went through, and the test failed with `Missing expected rejection`. kthreadd lives as long as the kernel and no unprivileged user can signal it. On macOS, or in a container where pid 2 is not a kernel thread, the tests still borrow the lowest-pid root process. Test-only: the reap and the open are unchanged.
- **A step's next attempt on a fenced durable-disk lease is refused at the recovery driver's flush**
  - A workflow run on a durable disk, wired as a host wires it (pi's workflow extension over `openDurableRun`), whose lease is fenced while a step's first attempt is out. The recovery driver may admit the next attempt in that process, since a fence is not final; the flush before it commits through the run's Harness over the lease's observed storage, so it is refused with the fence and the node runner is not called again. Two ways in: a failed heartbeat write, and a lease that lapsed while the process was stuck (the watchdog thread's lapse, which the lease finds at its next check or at that commit).
- **Each pi leg runs as five jobs, so a merge queue run takes about 8 minutes less**
  - `verify (<node>)` waits on every pi leg, and the leg that ran the agentrun-serve, pi-transport and pi-git-remote suites one after another took about 21 minutes: the longest job in the queue.
  - Each leg now runs as `pi-1-2 (<pi>, consumer)`: the packed packages installed in an empty directory, and the reference example; `first`: agentrun-serve, and the mid-stream kill and resume; `transport`: pi-transport and pi-git-remote; `browser`: pi-browser, pi-durable-disk and pi-durable-agent; `rest`: every other pi workspace, and any new one. Each suite still runs once on each pi version.
  - Estimated from the last queue runs: the longest leg drops to about 13 minutes, and a queue run from about 22 to about 14 minutes. The cost is six more jobs per run on standard runners, about 27 more runner-minutes, since each repeats the install and the build, and six more installs from the npm registry.
  - Risk: the same suites run on the same runners with the same installs; only the grouping changes. The required checks, `verify (22.19.0)` and `verify (22.23.2)`, keep their names.
- **Each pi leg runs as parallel jobs, and verify now fails unless every pi leg passed**
  - A pi leg ran every suite one after another, and took 25 to 30 minutes against its 30-minute limit: one 1.3.0 leg was cancelled while its suites were still passing.
  - `scripts/pi-leg.mjs` gains `--tests <names>` and `--tests-except <names>`. CI splits each leg into `pi-1-2 (<pi>, <suites>)` jobs with them. Each suite runs once, a new pi workspace runs in `rest`, and every job that runs suites installs and checks the whole family.
  - `verify (<node>)`, the required check, now needs the pi legs too. A leg that failed, was cancelled or was skipped fails it, so the merge queue waits on the pi legs. Every other side job stays advisory.
- pi-transport's launch tests now wait for a file's text, not only for the file, when the program writes that file through a shell redirect. The shell creates the file empty before the command writes it, so a test that polled until the file existed could read it empty. It then failed (`expected '' to be '<hash>'`), or checked an empty environment and passed on nothing. The waits for the hash of the program's stdin and secrets, its environment, its uid and its pid, and for the watchdog's pid, now wait until the file has text. The frame tests wait for the last file the second incarnation writes before they read the others. Test-only: the launcher and the box code are unchanged.
- `scripts/port-durable-harness.mjs` regenerates the code copied from vercel-labs/durable-harness into `@parcha/agentrun-kernel` from four inputs: the pristine upstream files at the pin, named patches (`vendor/durable-harness/port/patches/<upstream package>/NNN-<slug>.patch`, each with a `Reason:`, an `Upstream:` of `offer` or `ours`, and the tests it is `Pins:`-ed by), one import table (`port/imports.json`) and generated headers. A module or symbol the table lacks, or maps to a gap, fails the port by name; a patch applies with exact context or fails by name. `--check` (run by `npm run test:release`) fails on any byte of the committed `src/process` that the port does not make, and on a patch that changes nothing; `--drop <patch>` regenerates without one patch, for mutants; `--pin <sha> --upstream <clone>` reports an upgrade as the pristine diff, the table against the new text and the patches that fail. The copies' Changed-by line is now generated from the patch list, and the shared process layer's files are otherwise unchanged. The Codex harness files (`harness.ts`, `app-server.ts`, `rollout.ts`, `config.ts`, `binary.ts`, `test/fake-app-server.mjs`) regenerate the same way from six named patches and the one import table, whose entries name the package they apply to. The Claude Agent adapter's copied files (`src/{binary,env,harness,ids,inbox,store,transcript,versions}.ts` and the two scripted fakes under `src/testing/`) regenerate the same way from fourteen named patches; a relative import that lands in a file AgentRun wrote in place of upstream's is named in the one import table.
- serve's box tests plant the four keys a tester runs everything with (OpenRouter's, Monid's, Parallel's and Browserbase's), as well as an unnamed key and OpenAI's, each as its own sentinel value, and search the box's environment and `/proc` for every value. A key handed to a harness under another name, such as the OpenRouter key passed on as a model key, now fails the tests if it reaches a box. The faux-box, fake-Docker and real-Docker tests all change. Test-only: the server is unchanged.
- **serve's harness-run tests wait for a run as long as they allow themselves, and still report where it stopped**
  - Seven of the file's 60 s tests followed their run with `follow()`'s default 30 s deadline. Under load, a run that needed more than 30 s failed "the run did not reach completed" with half the test's budget unused.
  - Every `follow()` in the file now waits 55 s, under its test's budget, so a run that never finishes fails with follow's own "last <state>" line rather than the test's timeout.
  - The three tests that follow a run twice get 120 s, and the one that follows three runs in turn gets 180 s.
- serve's run-box tests wait for a destroyed box's container file to be gone as well as for its `rm` call. The fake `docker` writes a call's arguments before it acts on it, so under load a test could see the `rm` and then read the container still there, and the create-failure test's polling of the containers could read a file an `rm` in flight was removing. Test-only: the fake and the server are unchanged.
- The serve test for setup's count of a big home now checks that the count answers, then stops the server, instead of racing the server's exit against a 3.5 s bound while the count runs. The old test failed on loaded CI runners with correct code (an exit 3.5 s after SIGTERM), because the time a correct count takes to stop and the time a count that reads the sessions for keys takes overlapped. Its result also depended on when SIGTERM landed: a stop before the count's listing ended it before any scan. The home now holds one subagent file of about 20 MB under 5,000 hard-linked names. A count that lists the files answers in about a second. A count that reads them would read about 100 GB and does not answer within the test's 30 s, so the test fails on every run. The test also no longer holds its process open for the full bound after it passes. Test-only: the server is unchanged.
- **`npm test` runs the workspaces' tests several at once, and `npm run test:changed` runs only what a change reaches**
  - `npm test` runs up to 6 workspaces at once (a quarter of the cores), the slowest first, where it ran all 16 one after another. On a 48-core machine a full run goes from 17 minutes to about 4.5, the length of the slowest workspace (`@parcha/agentrun-serve`). Each workspace's output is printed whole when it ends, under a line with its result and time, and the run ends with a table of every workspace's result and time and exits nonzero when one failed. `AGENTRUN_TEST_JOBS=<n>` sets how many run at once; `AGENTRUN_TEST_JOBS=1` runs them one by one, in the old order, with their output live.
  - The script owns the machine's budget: at most one test file per core runs at once across all the workspaces (`AGENTRUN_TEST_TOTAL=<n>` sets another total, for a shared machine). Each workspace running gets its share, as `--test-concurrency` for a `node --test` script (one that sets a lower value keeps it), and as `TEST_CONCURRENCY` and `VITEST_MAX_WORKERS` otherwise. The default number of workspaces at once is a quarter of the total.
  - Ctrl-C stops every workspace's processes. A process a workspace's tests leave running when they end is killed, and the workspace's result line says so.
  - `npm run test:changed` runs the workspaces with a file changed since the merge base with `origin/main` (committed, staged, unstaged or new), plus every workspace that depends on one of them. A changed file outside the workspaces, such as `package-lock.json`, `tsconfig.base.json` or a script under `scripts/`, runs them all; Markdown files, `changes/` and `docs/` run none. It prints each workspace it picked and why. `npm run test:changed -- --base <ref>` compares with another base.
- **A test that reads a pid from a file can no longer signal its own process group by reading the file mid-write**
  - A test that waited for a pid file to exist and then read it could read it in the moment between the file being made and the pid landing in it. The empty read was taken as pid 0, and `process.kill(0, "SIGKILL")` killed the test's whole process group: npm, the test runner and every test file still running. The shard then ended with no summary and no failing test named.
  - serve's crash-mid-request cap tests and ci-shard's cancel test now wait until the file holds a whole pid, and their fixtures write the pid to a file beside it and rename it into place. pi-browser's ffmpeg-timeout test reads its stub's pid file the same way in its cleanup.
  - Before any signal, each of these tests refuses a pid that is not an integer above 1, or that is the test's own: the test fails with a message naming the pid, and nothing is signalled.
- A GitHub workflow with a key twice in one map (two `env:` under a job, say), or one that doesn't parse, now fails the release tests, naming the file, line and column. YAML loaders differ on a duplicate key: GitHub's keeps one of the two without a word. `node scripts/check-workflows.mjs [directory]` runs the same check by hand.
- `docs/AUTOQA.md`'s "Subsystems" section gains the tool plane's seam, how to run it alone with the model and every third party faked, and the checks a QA pass runs when the tool plane changes.
- `examples/decision-demos` holds five decision workflows that run on AgentRun Server with no agent loop: one document's validity from its page images (Jev), a batch of 20 documents through a map with a written report, a KYB check that crosses Parallel's web-grounded registry answer with Jev's reading of the documents, a merchant's category and prohibited-goods check from its web page, and one question to a jury of Jev and three OpenRouter Decisions models. The documents are for invented companies, labelled for scoring. `run.mjs` starts the server from the checkout (with `--allow-code-nodes`), makes the five agents and runs a use case on fakes of every provider, or live with `--live`; `validate.mjs` and `rehearse.mjs` check and rehearse them offline.
- `docs/AUTOQA.md` is the repository's QA baseline: how to build and start AgentRun Server for a QA pass, how to authenticate, the core catalog of checks (health, auth, agents, runs per harness and effort, spend cap, cancel, follow-up, crash and restart durability, web_fetch, workflows, stats, archive, tool keys, session import and the console), what each one proves, the model and host caveats, and the open findings to re-check each pass.
- `docs/AUTOQA.md`'s kernel subsection is filled in: its seam (the run routes and fields, the journal's request marks, the run directory, the adapter interface it calls, and what it takes from the tool plane and places), how to run it alone with no key on the faux model and the faux harness, and the six checks a QA pass runs when the kernel changed.
- `docs/AUTOQA.md`: the kernel's multi-tool check runs alone (`node --test test/harness-multi-tool.test.mjs` from `packages/serve`) now that #1363 is on main, the kernel's journal row names `REQUEST_CONTINUED`, and the known open findings mark three items fixed, each with the check that re-checks it: the claude-agent and codex multi-tool hang (#1363, kernel check 6), the console's New agent refused with 422 for call-only tools (#1357, console check 2), and tool keys listing unbound tools as unlocked (#1356, tool plane check 1).
- `docs/AUTOQA.md`'s Places subsection is filled in: the seam between boxes, the agent's home and files and the kernel (pi-transport's `Box` and `BoxProvider` with `mounts`, the safe extractor and sync layer, the checkpoint vault, box-made stores, bwrap, and serve's run files, start sweep, agent place and box registry), a recipe that runs it alone on fake boxes and the faux model, and the six checks a QA pass runs when it changed.
- `docs/AUTOQA.md` gains a Subsystems section: one subsection per subsystem (kernel, tool plane, places, console with import and analytics, learning loop), each naming its seam, a recipe that runs it alone with the others faked, and the checks a QA pass runs when it changed. The console, import and analytics subsection is filled in; the others are left to the teams that own them.
- Install and `npx` commands in the READMEs and docs name `@latest` instead of `@beta`. Releases publish under npm's `latest` tag, and `beta` was not moved with them: it still pointed at 0.1.0-beta.21 while `latest` was 0.1.0-beta.28, and `@parcha/pi-durable-agent`, `@parcha/pi-git-remote` and `@parcha/pi-agent-learn` had no `beta` tag at all, so their install lines failed. The harness demo's hint for a missing `@parcha/pi-transport` names `@latest` too. `@parcha/agentrun-harness` is not on npm yet (its `latest` is a placeholder), so its README says it is held and installs it from a packed tarball. The README's Render option gives measured memory: a Starter's 512 MB runs about 3 pi agents at once, and Claude Code or Codex runs want a 2 GB instance. The stranger check reads the install line as `@latest`, installs `@latest` with `--registry`, and walks the keyless examples against the registry's `latest`. The release guide drops the step that moved `beta` after a release; `beta` is kept only for older links, with the one-time command to bring it up to a release.
- The README and the setup skill (`docs/SKILL.md`) lead with one model key: `OPENROUTER_API_KEY`, set where AgentRun starts, gives pi, Claude Code and Codex agents each a current model, with no `AGENT_MODEL`, and the console's Settings → Tool keys → Model keys is where to add it, with no restart. The Docker and Render instructions drop `AGENT_MODEL` for the same reason; a provider's own key (`ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` or `OPENAI_API_KEY`) still works in its place.
- The README says what runs today: an agent runs on pi by default, or on Claude (the Claude Agent SDK) or Codex, and `GET /v2/capabilities` says which the server runs. Comments and fixture notes no longer use internal planning names or ticket numbers.
- The README's Render option gives the runs each instance admits now that a run is admitted by the memory its harness takes: a Starter's 512 MB runs 3 pi agents or 2 Codex runs at once, and a 2 GB instance 10 Codex runs. It says the image leaves Claude Code out for now (the Dockerfile removes the Claude Agent SDK and CLI), so Claude Code agents run with `npx @parcha/agentrun`. The old figures (650 MB a Claude Code run, 400 MB a Codex run) predate the chunked check of a harness binary.

## 0.1.0-beta.28 (2026-10-28)

- **The chat view draws a run's answer that is a list of flat records, and a pipe table in the agent's words, as tables**
  - An answer field that is a list of flat records (every value a scalar or a list of scalars) is a table: columns in the order the records first name them, a list of scalars as a comma list, a missing field as an empty cell. Its cells are plain text, never markdown. Any other answer keeps its JSON.
  - A markdown pipe table in the agent's words (thinking, said text, Muse bubbles) is a table whose cells keep the page's inline markdown. The first line that is not a row ends the table and stays words, so nothing the agent wrote is dropped. A table wider than its box scrolls inside the box; the page never scrolls sideways.
- `submitParameters(schema)` is exported: the input schema of a node runner's `submit` tool, the node's output schema under `value` with its local `$ref`s moved with it. The Pi runner uses it, and so does any other runner, so a node's schema behaves the same on every harness.
- `validateWorkflow(workflow, { tools })`: an opt-in list of the host's tool names. Given, a node that names a tool outside it is refused at validation with `NODE_FIELD_INVALID`, at that node's path, naming the tool: an `agent`, `decide`, `extract`, `report` or `artifact` node's `tools`, a `call` node's `tool` with `via: "tool"`, and the same inside a child workflow. Absent, validation is unchanged, and an unknown tool is still refused when its node runs. The options' type is exported as `ValidateWorkflowOptions`.
- When Pi starts with an `--tools` allowlist that leaves out `agentrun`, the session now shows a warning with the `pi --tools` line that enables the AgentRun tool. Before, Pi disabled the tool without a word. A workflow whose agent node needs a tool that is not active is now refused with the names of those tools, `Not registered: grep, find.`, instead of a refusal that names no tool. The README and the guide give Pi's stock default tools (`read`, `bash`, `edit`, `write`), the working `--tools read,grep,find,ls,agentrun` line, and `--approve` for `pi install ... -l` in a project that is not trusted yet.
- A Pi node's turn limit can be seen and raised. A first-run tester's read-only research node failed three times at the fixed 8 turns, with no way to raise it and no word of how far it got.
  - An `agent` node may carry `max_turns`, a positive integer: the most turns it asks its host for, per attempt. The engine checks its shape and hands it to `runNode` as `maxTurns`; it enforces nothing, and naming it changes the workflow's digest.
  - In the Pi extension, `runtimeLimits.nodeMaxTurns` keeps its meaning (the most turns any node gets; a host lowers it or sets `null`) and is the cap on a node's `max_turns`; its default goes from 8 to 40. A node that names no `max_turns` gets 20 turns when it can only read (no `bash`, `edit` or `write`, and no host tools), and 8 otherwise. Inspection refuses a node that asks for more than the cap, naming it. `describe` reports the two defaults as `nodeTurnDefaults`.
  - A node that reaches its turn limit is not tried again: `PiRunError` for a turn limit is `final`, so the recovery driver gives it no second attempt. Before, each failure ran a second fresh session with the same prompt and limit; the tester's three runs spent 2 sessions of 8 turns each, and the second never helped.
  - `pi_turn_limit` says how many turns the node used of its limit and its last tool call, and how to raise `max_turns`. `PiRunError` carries `maxTurns` and `lastTool` (the tool's name, never its arguments).
  - `/agentrun limits` shows the limits in force, and the turn limit of each node that runs in a Pi session (agent, decide, extract, report) in the current workflow and where it comes from.
- **A script run through a symlinked path now runs as the command**
  - `apps/viewer/build.mjs` and the other repository scripts, examples and test fixtures that run as commands decided they were the command by comparing `process.argv[1]` with their own URL. Node resolves a module's URL through symlinks and leaves `argv[1]` as given, so through a symlinked directory (macOS's temp directory is one) each one exited 0 having done nothing: a site build on a Mac built no viewer. They now check `import.meta.main`, which Node sets from 22.18 on 22 and from 24.2. `scripts/entry-points.test.mjs` fails on any script that compares the two again.
  - Node 23 and Node 24.0 to 24.1 have no `import.meta.main`, so there these scripts would exit 0 having done nothing. `scripts/check-node.mjs`, which `npm run build` and the demos run first, now refuses them: the repository needs Node 22.19 or newer on 22, or 24.2 or newer.
- A string that a field's own `enum` or `const` lists exactly is kept as a value. Before, the null-spelling repair (`nullSpellings`, `healNullSpellings`, `repairRecord` and the DSL's `normalizeStringNullsForSchema`) turned "None", "null" or "N/A" into JSON null whenever the field also admitted null, even when the schema named that string as one of the field's values. The same holds in any `anyOf` or `oneOf` branch at any depth and through a local `$ref`. The scalar repair (`parseStringifiedContainers`) also leaves a listed string alone: a `const: "null"` stays the string. A spelling the field does not list exactly ("none" where the enum says "None") is still repaired to null.
- **The issue-triage example's `--max-usd` is checked before every lead request, not only on a timer**
  - The cap was watched by a 250 ms timer alone, so a lead that answers quickly could start and finish many paid requests between two ticks, and a busy machine stretched the gap. The spend is now also read, as the spool stands, before each lead request (the lead's own and a research node's, including a retry), and no request is made once the known spend has reached the cap.
  - The timer stays as the backstop that ends a request already in flight. The overshoot is at most that one request, which the stop message already says.
  - The example's test bills its stub lead once per call instead of on a clock, and checks that the run ends within one call's cost of the cap.
- **The dependency inventory leaves out a third-party package's optional peer that nothing requires at runtime**
  - The lockfile records an optional peer when the repository installs it for its own development (a test runner an auth library names), and the inventory followed it and everything under it as if consumers shipped it, which put a dev-only bundler's MPL-2.0 CSS engine under release review. A third-party package's optional peer now ships only where a runtime dependency requires it. A workspace package's own optional peers (the host it runs in, an adapter's SDK) are still followed.
  - On this lock no package leaves the inventory: `undici` and `zod` drop the packages that reached them only through `openai`'s and `@anthropic-ai/sdk`'s optional peers from their "Used by" lists.
- The `/agentrun` extension takes Jev's provider from the environment, as the Jev adapter's `jevProviderFromEnv` does: with only `OPENROUTER_API_KEY` set, a workflow's Jev decisions (including `/agentrun demo live`) go to Jev through OpenRouter instead of being refused for a missing `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`. With both keys set, TypeSafe is used as before. The configuration refusal names both keys, and `/agentrun status` says whether Jev goes via TypeSafe or OpenRouter.
- The outer model can no longer pass off its own work as an AgentRun run. A first-run tester twice saw it do the research itself and answer "Complete" and "Supported, High confidence" with no workflow run and no Jev call.
  - Every `agentrun` run result carries a `receipt`, the run id that `/agentrun history` lists (the durable tool's `receipt` is its run key). The results of `inspect`, `dry-run` and `load` say `no workflow ran`.
  - In a session where the author skill loaded (through its command, or because the model read its SKILL.md itself) or the `agentrun` tool was called, the extension adds its own entry under each final reply, labeled as a host notice and not written by the model: the receipt with the run's model steps, Jev decisions and direct tool calls (for a resumed run, this attempt only; a scripted run says no model or Jev call was made), or `no workflow ran in this turn`. The model's reply is left as it wrote it. The workflow's name, which the model writes, is printed only when it is a plain name (letters, digits and a few marks, up to 80 characters), and inside quotes as `workflow "name"`; any other prints as `a workflow`, here and in `/agentrun history`. A reopened session starts without the notice until the skill loads or the tool is called again.
  - The host addendum tells the model to quote the receipt or say no workflow ran, and never to report its own reading or searching as a workflow result.
  - The author skill now forbids passing research done while authoring in as the workflow's input, calling a result verified or supported without a judge call in the run, giving a judge file names instead of the material it judges, and adding a code node only to format output (code forces `--trusted`).
- A `call` node's poll that runs out of time fails one way: `call node "<label>" poll exceeded its <n>s deadline after <k> check(s)`, a `timeout`, with one `effect.poll` event settled `"deadline"` for the last check started. `<k>` counts the checks started, including a last check that the deadline stops before its effect begins. Before, a wait that woke past the poll deadline (a slow timer, or a retry's backoff) and a check the poll deadline cut failed with the same text but no count, and sent no `"deadline"` event; which one a run got depended on how late its timers fired.
  - Each check sends exactly one `effect.poll` event. A check that does not settle now sends `settled: null` after its interval wait, just before the next check starts, instead of before the wait; its `elapsed_ms` is unchanged (the time when the check ended). A wait that ends at or past the poll deadline sends `settled: "deadline"` for that check instead. A cancel during the wait still reports the finished check as `null`.
  - A check the poll deadline cuts is counted, and its `effect.failed` message carries the same counted text.
  - A poll run under a recovery driver does not change, including the text without a count when the poll deadline cuts a check.
- **This repository's install resolves undici 8.12.2 under the disk SDK and pi-coding-agent**
  - Both pin undici 8.10.2 exactly. A root override for `undici@8.10.2` moves those copies to 8.12.2. pi-browser keeps its own undici 6, which a blanket `undici` override would also have moved. CI now checks that no undici in the install is below 8.12.2.
  - Overrides apply only in the project that runs the install, so a project that installs these packages gets 8.10.2 under them until those releases move their pins. To install 8.12.2 there, add the same override to your own `package.json`: `"overrides": { "undici@8.10.2": "8.12.2" }`.
- The release check's registry consumer runs `npm install` with `--loglevel=error`, so a caller that silenced npm (`npm run -s`, which sets `npm_config_loglevel=silent` for its children) still sees why the install refused. Before, the refusal still failed the check, but with an empty npm error.
- The merge queue runs the transport test shard on GitHub's runners, as it does the viewer shard. Blacksmith's kernels have no Yama ptrace_scope, and pi-transport's launcher refuses a launch with stdin or secrets on such a host, so the shard's launch tests failed there while the code was right.
- **The hero example's cost check allows the fold's 1e-8 rounding**
  - The fold rounds a run's cost to 1e-8, so a priced run's total can land a hair under the raw sum of the eyes' charges (0.009632 for 0.009632000000000002). Beat 3 compared them exactly and failed on such runs; it now allows the 1e-9 the look cost's check already allows, and a test plants those charges.
- **pi-durable-disk's live-prepare test no longer fails when another test changes the package directory**
  - The Daytona live helper packed the package by having tar walk its directory. Other tests write there while the suite runs (package.test.ts creates `.tmp/`), and tar fails a directory whose entries change while it reads them ("file changed as we read it"). The helper now packs a list instead: the files git tracks or would track, plus the built `dist/`. That is the same set of files as before, and a test packs it while another process churns an entry in the directory.
- **The package check accepts five more undici advisories on the copies the disk SDK and pi-coding-agent pin, until 2026-11-06**
  - The disk SDK (1.10.0) and @earendil-works/pi-coding-agent (1.4.0) still pin undici 8.10.2 exactly. Five advisories published by 2026-10-27 are fixed only in 8.12.2: GHSA-4w7w-5xrc-9h26, GHSA-2m5v-pch6-74hx and GHSA-qrxm-fqhp-jwvm (high), and GHSA-9fm3-97px-rjf6 and GHSA-55wc-3pg8-jfw6 (moderate). The existing undici exception now names them. It keeps the same two parents and the same 2026-11-06 expiry, and any other advisory, any copy elsewhere, or a date past the expiry still fails the check.
- The release script test where the registry consumer cannot meet its pins now says what npm did when it fails another way: npm's own error (code, signal, message and stderr), or that the install succeeded, and the pi-codemode version the consumer resolved.
- **The checkout's docs name the Node range its scripts accept**
  - The contributor guides, the tutorials, the demos' setup prompts and the examples run from a checkout now say Node 22.19 or newer on 22, or 24.2 or newer. That is the range `scripts/check-node.mjs` accepts: Node 23 and 24.0 to 24.1 have no `import.meta.main`. The published packages still need Node 22.19 or newer.
- The README credits the projects AgentRun builds on, each with its license and a link: Pi, Stagehand and the Browserbase SDK, the TypeSafe SDK, Archil's disk SDK, pi-vision's document readers, PDF.js, and the smaller libraries.
- The README says agents run on pi today, and that bringing your own harness comes next, starting with Codex.
- The README's plan for bringing your own agent now says Codex first, with more harnesses later.

## 0.1.0-beta.27 (2026-10-25)

- New subpath `@parcha/agentrun-dsl/author-references`: the author skill's five documents (`SKILL.md`, `language.md`, `workflow-format.md`, `jev-decisions.md`, `sdk-authoring.md`) as static strings in `AUTHOR_REFERENCES`. It imports nothing, so a browser or a Worker can carry the references without reading files or loading Node's builtins.
- `rederiveDecision` returns `null` for a route receipt with no branch answer and for a pick receipt with no pick answer, as its documentation says it does for a receipt that decided nothing. A route receipt without its branch answer and with an `unsure` rule made it throw a `TypeError`; without an `unsure` rule it returned a decision with an undefined branch. A pick receipt without its answer returned `{ index: -1, none: false }`. A receipt the interpreter records always carries its answer, so only a damaged or hand-built receipt changes.
- `rederiveDecision` returns `null` for a `verify` receipt whose rule has a field with a threshold but no yes/no answer for it, as it already does for a receipt that decided nothing. Before, a missing answer read as `NaN`, which is not below the threshold, so the field was skipped and the receipt re-derived as `{ accepted: true }` — accepting where the live run, which scores a missing answer as 0, would have doubted or unmet it. A field whose threshold is `null` (held empty and not judged) needs no answer and is unchanged. A receipt the interpreter recorded carries every answer, so only a damaged or hand-built receipt changes.
- The `/agentrun` command shows an error's own text only when the extension wrote that text for the operator: its own messages, `WorkflowStoreError` and `ExtensionServiceError`. Any other error shows the generic message. Before, it showed any error whose text began like one of the extension's messages (`Workflow`, `Code`, `SOP`, ...), so a host callback's own error starting that way reached the operator as written.
- The web example's server reads the agent home again whenever the store's head commit moves. It keyed its cache on the store file's mtime and size, which a commit can leave as they were (the size does not move, and a write inside one tick of the filesystem's clock keeps the mtime), so a run started right after the server's last read could answer 404 until the next write.
- `verify (<node>)` runs its suites as ten shards at once (`scripts/ci-shard.mjs`) and passes when every shard passed; the required check names are unchanged. Pushes run CI on `main` only (a pull request's branch runs on `pull_request`), and a new push to a pull request cancels its running checks.
- The release workflow builds AgentRun Server's container image from the reviewed tag once publication passes, and pushes `ghcr.io/parcha-ai/agentrun-server:<tag>` and `:beta` for linux/amd64 and linux/arm64, with an SBOM and max provenance stored with the image.
- The release workflow no longer runs `npm run check` on the tagged commit: it requires the `verify (<node>)` gates of `ci.yml`'s push run on `main` for that commit to have passed (`scripts/release-ci-gate.mjs`) and stops on anything else, then builds and runs the checks that make the published archives (generated files, author contract, package and source verification, preflight). It publishes in dependency layers (`releaseLayers` in `scripts/release-preflight.mjs`): each layer is published, then every package of it is verified against the registry at once, and a failure stops the release before the next layer.

## 0.1.0-beta.26 (2026-10-24)

- Use npm 11.21.0 for CI, source and release installs. Install vision CI's native dependencies from Ubuntu's HTTPS archive. Pin the workspace and Docker runtime installs to Zod 4.6.6, and document the application-root override for browser hosts. The reference kill ledger accepts a scan-only result with no browser session while rejecting duplicate creates, unreleased sessions and page operations without a session.
- Validation refuses parallel branches that write the same label. An unaliased agent, decide or extract node writes its result under its label, but the parallel disjointness check read only `as`, so two such branches with one label passed validation and then failed every run at the merge with `parallel_write_conflict`. They are now refused with `PARALLEL_WRITE_CONFLICT` before anything runs. `declaredWrites`, which hosts can call, now includes that label. A code node's patch keys are still known only when it runs, and the merge still refuses a collision there.
- An inspection that `WorkflowExtensionService` refuses now reaches the caller as an `ExtensionServiceError` with the service's `code` (`admission`, `invalid_workflow`, ...), its message prefixed `Cannot inspect workflow:` as before, and the refusal as its `cause`. Before, inspection rewrapped it as a plain `Error` with the message only, so a host could branch on the code of a refused run but not of a refused inspection. Any other inspection failure keeps its original error as its `cause`.
- The `agentrun` tool's description no longer tells the model that a run always restarts with no checkpoint resume. It says what the extension does: a run the session left interrupted or failed resumes from its journal when its draft, input, SOP and tools are unchanged, and any other run starts new.
- The package gate accepts the undici copies that the `disk` SDK and `@earendil-works/pi-coding-agent` pin at 8.10.2 for GHSA-xjj6-f33q-929c (high) and two moderate advisories on the same versions, until 2026-11-06. Neither upstream has a release with the fixed undici 8.12.2 yet. An audit exception can now name more than one parent.
- examples/web: `AGENT_BROWSER` is `local`, `browserbase` or `kernel`, with no default. A run of an agent with a browser tool and no setting is refused with `set AGENT_BROWSER (or --browser): local (no key; a Chrome on this machine), browserbase (BROWSERBASE_API_KEY, BROWSERBASE_PROJECT_ID) or kernel (KERNEL_API_KEY)`; Browserbase is no longer picked just because its key is set. `local` is the run's own Chrome (`CHROME_PATH`), `browserbase` needs `BROWSERBASE_API_KEY` (the project id stays optional), and `kernel` drives pi-browser's Kernel provider (`KERNEL_API_KEY`, optional `KERNEL_BASE_URL`) and keeps no sign-in between runs. `chrome` is accepted as `local` for one more release, with a notice. The README's first run sets `AGENT_BROWSER=local`, and a table lists the env and the network each value needs.
- `examples/web/agents/company-research`: an agent, Desk, whose research prompt depends on the kind of organisation. A base prompt and an overlay for each kind (a public company, a non-profit, a small retailer, an online store) and a generic one are kept as `agent.prompts` in the agent's own home, each overlay with the rule that loads it (`company_kind` is its option at 0.8 or more, from a Jev choice question). `edit-prompts.mjs` checks `prompts.json`, then has the home check the set as it would save it (its own size limits included), and saves it into a home with `setPrompts`, or prints what the home holds. `config-page.mjs` writes one static, script-free page of the base, the overlays with their rules, the generic, and a table of runs with Jev's choice and probabilities (both passes when the host looked the company up), the lookup, the prompt loaded, the outcome and a link to the brief. Eight companies, four clear and four ambiguous, are in `companies.json`. The tests run offline.
- The web example's server picks each run's prompt before the run starts, when its home keeps prompt variants (`--agent <dir>` makes a new home from that agent's `agent.json` and skills): `choosePrompt` of @parcha/pi-durable-agent asks Jev the home's question about the person's first message, through the model gateway's TypeSafe pass-through (`/typesafe`) or TypeSafe itself on `TYPESAFE_API_KEY`, and when Jev is unsure looks the name up once (a Browserbase web search with `BROWSERBASE_API_KEY`, and Wikipedia's keyless search and summary, both at once) and asks again, else the generic prompt. `startRun` records the choice, and `prompt.json` beside the run holds each pass, what the lookup read, the reason and the guide: the home's base prompt and the chosen variant as they were then, which the run's instructions get on every launch, however the home's variants change after. With a Browserbase key the agent can list `web_fetch` and `web_search`, through Browserbase's own Fetch and Search APIs, which open no browser session; an agent that lists no browser tool gets no browser, and start-run.mjs starts no Chrome for it.
- A new `@parcha/pi-durable-agent/workflow` subpath runs an agent's workflow revision: `checkWorkflowBody` checks a revision's body before a store commits it, `runWorkflowRevision` runs its workflow with agentrun-pi's durable workflow task on the run's own store and returns the outcome and the `carryBack` input (`{ state, summary }`), `readWorkflowRun` reads the run back, read-only, and `revisionGraph` draws a revision's graph and its diff from a workflow parent. The package now depends on `@parcha/agentrun-dsl` and `@parcha/agentrun-pi`; only these functions load them, so an agent with no workflow revision never does.
  agentrun-pi's readers `readJournal` and `durableDoc` take a new option, `{ immutable: true }`, which opens a store no process writes any more (an ended run's, with no write-ahead log beside it) as an immutable file, so the read makes no file beside it.
- The web example records a run: with `RECORD_DIR` set, the agent app writes an agentrun.recording/1 directory with the harness recorder from inside the run (every document's lines, none dropped, pi's live events and the frames the frame store keeps), and start-run.mjs finishes it once the run has exited, with the agent's `report/index.html` as `manifest.artifact`. The Browserbase key and project id, the browser profile's id, live views and any URL parameter that carries a credential (its name with its value: `token`, `key`, `apiKey`, a signature) are `[redacted]`, email addresses a page printed are `[email]`, a session's id is a label of its hash, a document's `liveView` is null, and the machine is scrubbed; the run and the finish both scan for every id. A replay (`writeReplay`) takes the same browser ids, credential parameters, email addresses and live views out of its rows, text files and records, by the one set of rules both use (`agent/redact.ts`). The agent answers in the chat; the site-report skill writes report files (`report/index.html`, one script-free page) only when the person asks for a report, and the host writes `report/remember.html`, the recording's artifact entry (what the agent keeps: its signed-in sites, its memory files and its runs, never a ref).
